```
- SLAC II or III degenerative arthritis
- Irreducible carpal malalignment

Technical Points
- Perform a dorsal (zigzag, lazy scaphoid, or longitudinal) approach centered at the Lister tubercle.
- Incise the extensor retinaculum along the third compartment.
- Open septa between 2nd and 3rd, 3rd and 4th, and 4th and 5th dorsal com partments; coagulate intraseptal arteries.
- If the posterior interosseous nerve (PIN) is intact, one may perform a proximal based, nerve- sparing capsulotomy Should the surgeon elect to excise the PIN, a “ligament sparing” approach (maintaining the DRC ligament) or a “win dow capsulotomy” (see Fig. 13.27 ) can be performed to expose the carpus.
- Reducibility is checked by traction or by direct manipulation with Kirschner wires as joysticks.
- Enter the scaphoid with a guide wire for the 2.7 mm cannulated drill at the point of insertion of the dSLIL.
- Direct the wire along the axis of the scaphoid aiming at the palmar tuberosity.
- Conﬁrm with ﬂuoroscopy and overdrill with the 2.7 mm cannulated drill
- Make a 1- cm palmar incision over the scaphoid tuberosity and release the FCR tendon sheath.
- Obtain a distally based 8- cm strip of tendon (2 to 3 mm).
- Retrieve the tendon strip from the dorsum using a wire or a tendon passer.
- Carve a transverse trough over the dorsum of the lunate with a rongeur (see Fig. 13.32C ).
- Insert a 2- 0 nonabsorbable suture anchor into the lunate.
- Loop the tendon strip through the DRC near its triquetral insertion.
- While tensioning the tendon using the DRC as a pulley, transﬁx the SL and SC joints (two 0.062- inch Kirschner wires across the SL joint and one across the SC joint) (see Fig. 13.32E ). Bury wires below the skin.
- With full tension on the graft, weave the anchored 2- 0 suture through the tendon graft and secure it within the lunate cancellous trough with the suture anchor.
- Anchor the tail of the graft to the distal scaphoid.
- Close the capsule over the tendon strip securely.
- Repair the extensor retinaculum, and transpose the EPL.

![[Fig.13.27.png]]
> **Fig. 13.27** A, The “window” approach to the carpus preserves all dorsal ligaments while yielding clear visualization of the carpus.131 Note the capsuloligamentous attachments of the DRC and DIC to the lunate. B, The addition of a midcarpal window yields visualization of the distal carpal row and the distal scaphoid, as well as inspection of the DIC and its underlying DST ligament subcomponent insertions on the proximal carpal row.

![[Fig.13.32.png]]
> **Fig. 13.32** A 32-year-old lawyer sustained an injury to his left wrist while playing soccer 8 months ago. A, Posteroanterior (PA) radiograph showing relatively shortened scaphoid with mildly increased scapholunate (SL) interval. B, The scaphoid viewed from the dorsum could easily subluxate because the entire SL ligamentous complex was ruptured and could not be repaired (static reducible SL dissociation). C, A transverse trough was created on the dorsal aspect of the lunate. To ensure that the tendon would be permanently in contact with the lunate cancellous bone, an anchor suture was placed in the trough. D, A strip of flexor carpi radialis was harvested at the palmar side but left attached distally. Its proximal end was passed through a tunnel emerging at the distal-medial corner of the scaphoid. E, The tendon used the dorsoradial triquetrum ligament as an anchor point for adequate tension before being sutured onto itself. Two Kirschner wires were used to immobilize the reconstructed ligament for 6 weeks, with 4 more weeks in a removable splint. F, PA view obtained 8 months postoperatively. The patient had resumed his former sport activity at 6 months. At 18 months, the wrist remained stable and pain-free with 90% grip strength and 85% motion compared with the contralateral side.

Postoperative Treatment
- Short- arm thumb spica cast for 8 weeks; change at 10 days for stitch removal and radiographs.
- Removal of wires at 8 weeks and protection in a removable splint for an addi tional 4 weeks.
- Rehabilitation after cast removal to regain motion and grip strength.
- Contact sports not allowed until 6 months postoperatively.
```

Seeking improved functional and radiographic results and recognizing the limitations of a predominantly dSLIL reconstruction, several authors have proposed modifications of the technique to address the dorsal and volar components of the SLIL simultaneously.

Henry presented the technique and outcome of a 31- year- old active male treated 9 months postinjury for stage 4 SLD using a 3 mm strip of a distally based FCR tendon graft to reconstruct the dorsal and volar SL ligaments (Fig. 13.33). The graft is passed through a similar scaphoid tunnel as used in the three- ligament tenodesis (TLT) and redirected to the proximal and ulnar side of the lunate to pass through a dorsal- volar lunate tunnel. It is grasped through a small volar incision, tensioned, and tied to the native FCR tendon. The construct is stabilized with Kirschner wires and a short- arm cast for 8 weeks, when wires are removed and rehabilitation commenced. At 8 years postoperatively, carpal realignment was maintained, grip strength was reported at 96% and flexion- extension ROM at 98% of the opposite side, and DASH score of 7. Though not an anatomic reconstruction, the author proposes that dorsal and volar SL reconstruction is necessary for normal carpal mechanics. Outcomes of a larger series of patients would help define indications and limitations.

![[Fig.13.33.png]]
> **Fig. 13.33** Schematic representation using FCR tendon graft to reconstruct dorsal and volar scapholunate ligaments. (From Henry M. Reconstruction of both volar and dorsal limbs of the scapholunate interosseous ligament. J Hand Surg Am 38[8]:1625–1634, 2013.)

Expanding on this idea, Corella et al. recommend an arthroscopiccontrolled technique to reconstruct both the dorsal and palmar SL ligaments that obviates the potential destabilizing effects of a capsulotomy (Fig. 13.34). Two small incisions are placed at each end of the scaphoid, and a strip of FCR is passed through a tunnel similar to the one used for the TLT. A second dorsopalmar tunnel is drilled across the lunate through a second small incision, and the tendon graft is passed dorsalto- palmar. To facilitate this, a tendon passer is introduced between the FCR and the median nerve and used to retrieve the graft and tension it while it is being secured by two interference screws—one in the lunate and the other in the scaphoid. Once in the volar surface of the lunate, the remnant of the tendon graft is reattached to the volar surface of the proximal scaphoid to augment the volar component of the SL ligament. In 2017, Corrella and colleagues reported on 27 patients (EWAS grade IIIC in 14 patients, 13 grade IV lesions) and at 6 months noted improvements in pain and DASH scores after surgery. Longerterm outcomes studies are needed to assess the efficacy of this method.

![[Fig.13.34.png]]
> **Fig. 13.34** Arthroscopically guided tendon reconstruction of both dorsal and volar scapholunate (SL) ligaments using a strip of flexor carpi radialis tendon (arrow), as described by Corella et al.98

In 2015, Ho and colleagues reported 4- year outcomes of an arthroscopic assisted “box” technique that reconstructed the dorsal and volar SL ligaments. The authors utilize a palmaris longus tendon graft passed through dorsopalmar holes in the scaphoid and lunate, in a path superficial to the volar capsule and tied over the dorsal capsule in a “shoelace” fashion. The joint is stabilized with buried Kirschner wires for up to 8 weeks. At 48 months, 11 of the 17 patients reported no pain, 13 returned to their occupation, and the average SL gap was 2.9 mm. One patient developed scaphoid ischemia that did not advance. The indications, technical details, and critical points are elaborated in Chapter 17.

Kakar and Greene developed an open modification of the volar/dorsal box technique that augments the palmaris tendon graft with a synthetic tape to double the failure strength of the reconstruction (Fig. 13.35). Potential benefits of the SL ligament 360- degree tenodesis (SL 360) procedure (Video 13.3) include the lack of use of Kirschner wires and the potential for earlier rehabilitation compared with traditional immobilization. Biomechanical analysis of this technique noted that the 360- degree tendon reconstruction had a breaking strength of 143 N whereas adding a synthetic tape increased it to 283 N, which is close to the native ligament’s 300 N breaking strength. A dorsal approach is performed, a complete SL injury is confirmed, and confirmation of a reducible carpus performed by traction or by direct manipulation with Kirschner wires as joysticks. To aid in scaphoid reduction, a Kirschner wire is placed in the distal pole of the scaphoid that can be used to extend and supinate the carpus. This Kirschner wire hole will be used at the end of the procedure to anchor the dorsal limb of the free tendon graft to resist scaphoid flexion. An extended carpal tunnel approach is then made to expose the palmar carpus. Prior to drilling the bone tunnels, it is *critical* that the Kirschner wires be precisely inserted in the scaphoid and lunate and confirmed under fluoroscopy to prevent tunnel fracture.

![[Fig.13.35.png]]
> **Fig. 13.35** Intraoperative photos demonstrating the SL 360-degree procedure. A, The graft has been passed from dorsal to volar through the lunate and volar to dorsal through the scaphoid to reconstruct the volar SL ligament. B, Demonstrating the new volar SL ligament. C, The graft has been passed back through the lunate, thereby reconstructing the dorsal SL ligament. Note the Kirschner wires in the distal scaphoid and dorsoulnar aspect of the lunate to permit application of a reduction clamp to reduce the SL joint diastasis. D, Implants have been placed from dorsal to volar in the lunate and scaphoid to secure the tendon graft and permit passage of the reinforcing synthetic tape through its cannulations. E, The dorsal tail of the tendon graft has been secured with a suture anchor to the distal pole of the scaphoid to help restrain scaphoid flexion. During capsular closure, note the suture has been passed from the carpus through the dorsal capsule to reinforce the DIC and DRC attachments. F, Reconstruction of the long radiolunate (long RL) ligament (black arrow) using the free end of the graft that’s been passed through the lunate and the synthetic tape anchored to the origin of the long RL on the radius. (B and D, From Kakar S, Greene RM. Scapholunate ligament internal brace 360-degree tenodesis [SLITT] procedure. J Wrist Surg 7[4]:336–340, 2018; A, C, E, F, Copyright © Mayo Foundation for Medical Education and Research and Sanjeev Kakar. All rights reserved.)

Within the lunate, the Kirschner wire should be centrally located, parallel to its long axis, and sufficiently proximal to ensure that the 3 mm cannulated drill will not violate the midcarpal articular surface. The scaphoid Kirschner wire should be parallel to the lunate Kirschner wire and should not penetrate the dorsal oblique ridge to preserve scaphoid vascularity. Prior to creating the bone tunnels in the scaphoid and lunate, the appropriate drill bits can be placed over the Kirschner wire and checked under direct vision and with fluoroscopy to ensure there is at least 3 mm of bone around the proposed bone tunnels to prevent iatrogenic fracture. The wires are over-drilled with a 3 mm (lunate) and a 2.5 mm (scaphoid) cannulated drill using saline irrigation. If the patient has DISI, the lunate is corrected to a neutral position before tunnel placement, by either pushing down onto the capitate, placing a Kirschner wire into the lunate to act as a joystick, or flexing the wrist to obtain a normal RL angle and fixing the lunate with a temporary RL pin. Care should be taken to pass the pin so as not to interfere with the lunate bone tunnel. The scaphoid Kirschner wire and bone tunnel is angled distal dorsal- to- proximal volar such that the bone tunnels converge in the coronal, sagittal, and axial planes. A 2 to 2.5 mm free tendon graft (e.g., palmaris longus) is passed from dorsal to volar through the lunate and volar to dorsal through the scaphoid, thereby reconstructing the volar SL ligament (see Fig. 13.35A and B). The free end of the graft is passed back through the lunate tunnel from dorsal to volar, reconstructing the dorsal SL. To aid in SL joint reduction, a Kirschner wire is placed within the dorsum of the triquetrum. A reduction clamp is then applied to this Kirschner wire and the one placed within the distal pole of the scaphoid (see Fig. 13.35C). The graft is pulled under maximal tension and the wrist cycled through flexion- extension arcs to remove any further creep. Cannulated biotenodesis screws are then prepared for placement by removing the implant from the driver and wetting it to ease disengagement when implanted. The implants are placed from a dorsal approach into the scaphoid and lunate (see Fig. 13.35D). To help resist scaphoid flexion, the dorsal limb of the free end of the graft is secured with an anchor to the distal pole of the scaphoid (see Fig. 13.35E). To eliminate the need for Kirschner wires, a 1.3 mm nonabsorbable synthetic tape is passed from dorsal to volar through the screw cannulations in the scaphoid and lunate implants, tied palmarly, and cut (see Fig. 13.35D). It is important to pass the synthetic tape through the screw cannulation to help prevent it cutting through the bone tunnels. The DIC and DRC ligaments are repaired back down to the carpus during capsular closure to the soft tissue on the carpus or with the use of suture anchors (see Fig. 13.35E). The outcomes of a patient 13 months postoperatively were reported, with improvements in pain and grip strength and restoration of normal radiographic alignment. A case was reported using a modification of the procedure to augment the long RL ligament with the tail of the volar graft and synthetic tape for ulnar translocation of the lunate.In a recent three- center study, Kakar and colleagues reported early outcomes of the SL 360 technique with synthetic tape augmentation in 9 patients for reducible SL instability. Eight of the 9 patients did not require Kirschner wire stabilization. After a mean of 33.7 months’ follow- up (12 to 51.3 months), there was improvement in clinical, radiographic, and functional outcomes. Visual analog scores decreased from 7 preoperatively to 1, Mayo Wrist scores improved from 44 to 80, PRWE from 58 to 12, and quick- DASH from 54 to 12. Radiographically, the SL gap decreased from 5.1 to 2.8 mm and SL angle from 71 to 57 degrees. As similarly reported by Ho et al., there was one patient who developed lunate ischemia that remained stable during follow- up. While the data is encouraging, longer- term clinical studies are needed to further validate these techniques.

It is important to emphasize that the use of a tendon graft to stabilize unstable segments of the wrist is feasible but not ideal. The elastic properties of a tendon will never match those of a ligament. Also, the resultant neoligament probably will never contain mechanoreceptors, so it will not participate in the sensorimotor system to activate muscle stabilization of the joint. Finally, extreme care must be exercised when planning and drilling tunnels through these small and poorly vascularized bones.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Dorsal and Volar Scapholunate Ligament Reconstruction and SL 360 Technique (see Video 13.3)

Indications
- Dynamic complete SLD, reducible, with normal cartilage (stage 3)
- Static complete SLD, reducible, with normal cartilage (stage 4)

Preoperative Evaluation
- Routine radiograph examination (both wrists)
- Bilateral clenched- ﬁst view
- Fluoroscopic evaluation of abnormal carpal motion
- The carpus, and in particular the scaphoid and lunate, are evaluated with MRI to ensure adequate size to accommodate the bone tunnels and rule out edema to mitigate the risk of fracture or avascular necrosis.
- Arthroscopy or MRI to conﬁrm location of SL injury, rule out cartilage degen eration or scaphoid/lunate edema, LT injury, and to assess injury to other critical ligaments

Contraindications
- Stage II or III SLAC arthritis
- Arthroﬁbrosis
- Chronic regional pain syndrome (CRPS)
- Fixed, irreducible lunate malalignment
- Edema within the scaphoid and/or lunate noted on the preoperative MRI (rel ative contraindication)

Technical Points
- A dorsal longitudinal approach is utilized and the extensor retinaculum incised over the third compartment.
- Develop radial and ulnar-based extensor retinacular ﬂaps to expose the sec ond to ﬁfth extensor compartments.
- If a PIN neuroma exists, a 2 cm segment is excised and a ligament sparing or window capsulotomy performed.
- Reducibility is checked by traction or by direct manipulation with Kirschner wires as joysticks.
- Once a complete and reducible SL ligament injury is conﬁrmed, an extended carpal tunnel incision is created to expose the volar carpus.
- A 2 to 2.5 mm tendon graft is harvested and whip stitched at either end and placed within a tensioning guide to remove creep from within the tissue.
- Extreme care needs to be paid when placing the Kirschner wires for bone tunnel creation. The wires must be appropriately centered within the lunate and scaphoid to guard against tunnel fracture and their position conﬁrmed under live ﬂuoroscopic imaging as well as under direct inspection.
- Liberal saline irrigation is used when creating the bone tunnels. When pass ing the Kirschner wires and making the bone tunnels, the contents of carpal
```

SL reconstruction using an “axis” method. Based on the observation that some patients with failed SL fusions did well clinically, the RASL procedure was proposed as a novel approach to static SL instabilities. Before repairing the ligament remnants, the articular cartilage between the scaphoid and lunate is removed and the joint is reduced and transfixed with one headless screw for 12 months or more. The goal is to create a fibrous and durable link of the SL joint while enabling rotation of each bone around the screw. Early results were encouraging, with most patients exhibiting excellent ROM and a strong grip. When performed arthroscopically in 18 patients, results at a mean of 3 years postoperative demonstrated a pain score of 2.5, DASH score of 8, and 84% grip strength compared with the contralateral side. Six patients had SL widening, 1 had screw loosening, and 2 developed SLAC arthritis. The authors noted that patients with a SL gap greater than 5 mm or who had grade 1 tunnel need to be protected by intermittent retraction rather than continued retraction, especially on the median nerve. Use a rongeur to create a trough for the dSLIL graft. Using a tendon passer through the bone tunnels, the tendon graft is passed from the lunate through the scaphoid and back through the lunate in a 360- degree conﬁguration with care taken to protect and retract the volar structures. Compression of the SL joint is applied with a reduction clamp. Pull maximum tension on the graft and cycle the wrist through ﬂexion extension. Prior to inserting the cannulated biotenodesis screws, the screws are removed from the driver to ensure they will easily disengage from the driver when inserted into the bone tunnels. Cannulated biotenodesis screws are then placed from dorsal to volar through the scaphoid (2.5 mm) and lunate (2.5 mm or 3.0 mm), in that order. The dorsal limb of the graft is anchored to the distal pole of the scaphoid. The 1.3 mm synthetic tape is shuttled through the biotenodesis screws and tied palmarly. On the palmar side, one limb of the synthetic tape and the tendon graft may be anchored to the radius to augment the long RL. The procedure obviates the need for Kirschner wires, but should the surgeon decide to place them for additional stability, SC Kirschner wires can be added. The DIC and DRC ligaments are repaired back down to the carpus upon cap sular closure either to the soft tissue on the carpus or via mini anchors.

A short- arm thumb spica splint for 2 weeks after which the sutures are removed. If Kirschner wires have been placed, a short- arm thumb spica cast is placed for an additional 4 weeks after which the patient is placed into a thumb spica removal splint. Kirschner wires are removed at 9 weeks after surgery, if placed. If no Kirschner wires have been placed, a short- arm spica cast is placed for an additional 2 to 4 weeks. Under hand therapy supervision, wrist proprioception and gradual ROM exer cises are started. Strengthening exercises are initiated at 12 weeks.

A three- center series of 9 patients with a mean follow of 33 months reported

SL gap 2.8 mm, PWRE 12, and quick- DASH 12. Longer- term clinical studies are needed to validate this technique.

SLAC changes had a higher complication rate. Larson and Sternreported that the screw often became loose, with deteriorating outcomes over time. Carpal collapse recurred when the screw was removed. Eight wrists were followed for a mean of 38 months, and the authors reported radiographic failure in 5 patients by 5 months postoperative. All patients followed a postoperative protocol that included cast immobilization for 6 weeks and strengthening programs beginning at 10 to 12 weeks. Screw trajectory has been shown to be critically important for this technique in a biomechanical study. Koehler et al. noted that the lateral aspect of the dorsal scaphoid ridge proved to be a suitable reference landmark when placing the screw. To avoid these complications, Fok and Fernandez added a dorsal ligament reconstruction to the RASL procedure and reported satisfactory outcomes in 36 patients (25 with static instability) at an average of 7.9 years (Fig. 13.36).

![[Fig.13.36.png]]
> **Fig. 13.36** Reduction-association of the scapholunate joint, the so-called RASL procedure, has been suggested to avoid the problem of recurrent diastasis after an SL ligament repair.144 Fernandez has reported good results by combining the RASL procedure with an extensor carpi radialis tenodesis, as illustrated in this figure.165 ERCL, Extensor radialis carpi longus; RASL, reduction association of the scapholunate.

Similar to the RASL procedure, other techniques employ a tendon graft placed down the “axis” of the SL joint to link the two bones. In the scapholunotriquetral (SLT) technique, Ross et al. utilized a distally based FCR tendon strip to reduce scaphoid dorsal translation, rotatory subluxation, and SL alignment by passing it through the center of the SL articulation (Fig. 13.37). Two tunnels are utilized: The scaphoid tunnel is similar to that of the TLT, and the second tunnel is drilled across the midportion of the lunate’s articular surface and through the triquetrum, emerging through a counterincision on the triquetrum’s ulnar border. In this way, the tendon graft is placed near the so- called axis of the SL and triquetrolunate joints. The tendon graft is secured using an interference screw in the triquetrum. A portion of the DIC is then reconstructed with the tail of the tendon graft. A prospective series of 11 patients demonstrated good early radiologic and clinical outcomes at 12 months.

![[Fig.13.37.png]]
> **Fig. 13.37** Schematic representation of the bone tunnels used in the scapholunotriquetral tenodesis introduced by Ross et al. A distally based FCR graft is tunneled through the scaphoid to emerge at the dorsal and ulnar aspect of the proximal pole. A second tunnel is made across the lunate and triquetrum, beginning at the midportion of its scapholunate articular surface and across the “axis” of both bones. The tendon graft is passed through the axis tunnel and tensioned to reduce the scapholunate joint. The repair is completed by passing the graft dorsally to reconstruct the dorsal intercarpal ligament attachments to the lunate and scaphoid. (Redrawn from Ross M, Loveridge J, Cutbush K, Couzens G. Scapholunate ligament reconstruction. J Wrist Surg 2[2]:110–115, 2013.)

The authors of the SLAM technique (ScaphoLunate Axis Method) propose placing a graft through the “axis” of SL rotation so as to act as a flexible biological link between the two bones while allowing scaphoid and lunate rotation. Mechanical testing showed favorable correction of SL gap and angle compared with the TLT and Blatt techniques.Early results of 13 patients at 11 months were satisfactory, with 62% grip strength and closure of the SL gap. Chan et al. reported one case of avascular necrosis of the lunate using this technique.

A theoretical concern among each of the techniques that uses an implant or a tendon graft to recreate a fixed “axis” between the two bones is that the anatomic axis for SL rotation lies largely outside the bony contours and changes dramatically depending on direction of rotation. The ability of axis or “association” techniques to correct DISI was questioned in the laboratory; a recent study demonstrated that the RASL repair, while statistically superior to augmented tendon graft reconstructions in closing the SL gap, was statistically inferior at correcting abnormal RL or SL angles.

Before deciding on any of the preceding techniques for SL instability, it is imperative not to forget that stability cannot be built on an unstable foundation. If the lunate is unstable and in DISI, reestablishing the SL ligament connections alone will not solve the problem. Indeed, for the techniques just discussed to be successful, the lunate must be stable and normally aligned (Fig. 13.38). If it is not, the options described later for stage 5 are recommended.

![[Fig.13.38.png]]
> **Fig. 13.38** Tendon reconstructions of scapholunate (SL) dissociations can only be effective if the lunate is stable. An associated ulnar translation of the carpus will lead to failure. When evaluating an SL dissociation, therefore, it is imperative to rule out this problem, as emphasized by Taleisnik in 1988. The red arrow indicates subluxation of the scaphoid and the yellow arrow indicates ulnar translocation of the lunate.

**Stage 5: scapholunate dissociation with rotatory subluxation and dorsal intercalated segment instability; reducible with normal cartilage.** As stated earlier, rupture of both the SL interosseous and one or more of the critical ligament stabilizers will result in postural changes of both the scaphoid and the lunate. In general, it is accepted that an unstable segment cannot be stabilized using another unstable segment.

If the lunate is unstable, reconstructions of the SL ligaments that do not address its instability are bound to fail. DISI is defined by a lunate angle greater than 15 degrees of extension. Prior to considering a particular surgical technique, an appreciation of the “critical stabilizers” of the proximal carpal row, a comprehensive understanding of which ligaments are deficient, and the use of a technique that reconstructs those ligaments to correct and sustain normal RL angle, is essential for success. This is the rationale for tendon graft techniques that are designed to reconstruct not only the interosseous ligament but the critical stabilizers of the proximal carpal row. Correcting sagittal or coronal plane lunate malalignment represents one of the most difficult challenges in carpal instability.

Spiral tenodesis. As described in the kinetics section of this chapter (see page 496), with axial load the scaphoid flexes and pronates under the influence of the inclined trapezium and trapezoid, and the distal row pronates in turn around the LC joint. In the case of SLD, the unstable scaphoid translates dorsally over the lip of the radius and painfully reduces in wrist extension. In stage 5 SL ligament dissociation, the lunate and triquetrum rotate into DISI, and the intercalated segment tends to translocate ulnarly down the inclination of the radius due to the capitate axial load. To constrain all of these displacements, a number of ligaments need to be structurally and functionally intact, including the long and short RL, the DRC, the dorsal SL, the dorsal intercarpal, and the DST ligaments. Except for the DRC ligament, all these structures spiral around the central column so as to prevent excessive pronation of the distal row under load. The so- called antipronation spiral tenodesis, described by Chee et al. in a case report, was developed to reconstruct this spiral arrangement of ligaments using a strip of FCR autograft.

Technique: spiral tenodesis. The technique begins as for the TLT by tunneling the graft through the scaphoid and anchoring it to a trough in the lunate. However, instead of threading the tendon graft through the DRC, the FCR is threaded through a dorsopalmar tunnel across the triquetrum to exit palmarly at the floor of the carpal tunnel. (Fig. 13.39A). From there, it is brought under the flexor tendons and retrieved through a separate incision near the radial styloid, where it is inserted with a bone anchor or tunnel. This procedure is only indicated if the instability is easily reducible and there is no cartilage damage. In the case report by Chee, at 34 months postsurgery, the patient had minimal pain when performing heavy duties with a grip strength of 85% of the contralateral side. A larger clinical series of these procedures has not been reported.

![[Fig.13.39.png]]
> **Fig. 13.39** A, Schematic representation of the so-called antipronation spiral tenodesis (arrows) described in a case report by Chee et al.172 It is recommended for reducible scapholunate dissociation with instability of the radiolunate articulation. B to F, ECRL spiral tenodesis. B, Showing passage of the ECRL from volar to dorsal through to the proximal and dorsal aspect of the scaphoid. Note the scaphoid is in the flexed position. C, By pulling on the graft, the scaphoid is taken out of its flexed and pronated position and has been reduced to its anatomic position. D, Reconstruction of the dorsal SL ligament. E, Preoperative radiograph demonstrating SL joint diastasis. F, By 17 months postreconstruction, the carpus is well aligned. (B, C, E, From Kakar S, Greene RM, Garcia-Elias M. Carpal realignment using a strip of extensor carpi radialis longus tendon. J Hand Surg Am 42[8]:667.e1–667.e8, 2017.)

Kakar et al. presented a case report of a modification of the spiral tenodesis procedure, intended to create a more robust reconstruction of the STT ligament by using a strip of the ECRL tendon instead of the FCR (see Fig. 13.39B–F). Like the spiral tenodesis, it is intended specifically to treat stage 5 SLD, perilunate injuries, and reducible ulnar translocations of the lunate. Through a dorsal approach, a distally based 3 mm strip of the ECRL tendon is tunneled through the STT joint capsule from dorsal to palmar. The graft is then passed around the lateral aspect of the distal scaphoid, and through an oblique 2.7 mm tunnel from the lateral aspect of the scaphoid tuberosity to the dorsal and proximal aspect of the scaphoid (see Fig. 13.39A). Tension on the graft will reduce the scaphoid from its flexed and pronated position (see Fig. 13.39B), and its vector will reconstruct the important palmar radial STT ligaments. Kirschner wires are placed across the reduced SL and SC joints, and the ECRL graft is secured to the dorsal aspect of the decorticated lunate with a suture anchor, thereby reconstructing the dSLIL (see Fig. 13.39C). An extended carpal tunnel release is performed and a 2.7 mm bone tunnel is created through the triquetrum through which the ECRL graft is passed from dorsal to volar, deep to the flexor tendons and median nerve, and secured to either the radial styloid with an anchor or bone tunnel. If deemed necessary, a portion of the ECRL can be anchored across the volar aspects of the lunate and scaphoid, thereby reconstructing the volar SL ligament. The patient is placed into a thumb spica cast for 8 to 10 weeks after which the Kirschner wires are removed and a supervised rehabilitation course is started emphasizing FCR and ECRB strengthening and avoiding ECU activation (see Fig. 13.39D–F). At 17 months after surgery, the index patient returned to full firefighting duty with a DASH score of 14 and grip strength of 76% of the uninjured wrist.

Anatomic front and back repair. Sandow and Fisher published a case series of 10 patients with a minimum of 24 month follow- up treated with a FCR autograft/synthetic reconstruction for chronic SLD with or without DISI (stage 4 or 5). The anatomic front and back (ANAFAB) procedure was conceptualized using a rules- based motion concept of carpal kinematics, and it reconstructs the radiopalmar STT and long RL stabilizers of the proximal carpal row, in addition to the dorsal SL and dorsal intercarpal ligaments (Fig. 13.40). The procedure augments the repair with synthetic tape and bone anchors, thereby obviating the need for Kirschner wires. In a recent cadaveric biomechanical study, Burnier et al. compared the ANAFAB technique to the TLT and RASL repairs in a model of Stage 5 SL dissociation with DISI and dorsal scaphoid translation. The ANAFAB repair was the only repair that consistently corrected both increased lunate extension and dorsal scaphoid translation.

![[Fig.13.40.png]]
> **Fig. 13.40** The ANAFAB (anatomic front and back) procedure simultaneously reconstructs the radiopalmar STT, the dorsal scapholunate, dorsal intercarpal, and the long radiolunate ligaments, while avoiding the need for Kirschner wires by augmenting the repair with synthetic tape. A, Preoperative lateral and PA pronated pencil grip radiographs of a 39-year-old male, demonstrating scaphoid rotatory subluxation, scaphoid dorsal translation (yellow arrow), DISI, and scapholunate diastasis. B, Anterior and posterior schematic views of the completed ANAFAB repair.173 C, 3-mm bone tunnels drilled through the (left to right) scaphoid, lunate, and distal radius. Note the lunate tunnel is angled proximal to distal to aid in correction of DISI. D, The dorsal scapholunate ligament reconstruction (black arrow) with tendon/synthetic tape composite. E, The long radiolunate ligament reconstruction with tendon/synthetic tape composite. F, 5-month postoperative lateral and pencil grip stress films demonstrating reduction of dorsal scaphoid translation (yellow arrow), rotatory scaphoid subluxation, DISI, and scapholunate diastasis. (Clinical images copyright © Scott W. Wolfe, MD; schematics copyright © Michael J. Sandow, FRACS.)

Technique: ANAFAB. Similar to the TLT procedure, dorsal longitudinal and palmar “hockey- stick” incisions are necessary for exposure (Video 13.4). The SL joint is exposed using a 3 cm radiocarpal “window” capsulotomy that spares the DIC and DRC ligaments (see Fig. 13.27A). A 2.5 mm by 15 cm distally based portion of the FCR tendon is prepared and combined with two strands of synthetic tape anchored in the trapezium. The construct is tunneled through the scaphoid to emerge dorsally near the dSLIL insertion. The construct is then passed dorsal to volar through the lunate, tangential to the midcarpal subchondral bone plate, and exits distal and slightly ulnar on the lunate palmar surface, to reconstruct the dSLIL. The tendon construct is retrieved by extending the FCR incision and exposing the palmar carpus, deep to the tendons and median nerve. It is then passed from the insertion of the long RL ligament on the palmar rim of the radius to the Lister tubercle dorsally to reconstruct the long RL. Maximal tension is applied to the construct proximally at the Lister tubercle, while fluoroscopy is utilized to ensure closure of the SL interval, as well as the simultaneous restoration of normal SL and RL alignment. The entire tendon/tape construct is secured to the radius under maximal tension with a 3.5 mm bone anchor proximal to the Lister tubercle, with an additional reinforcing anchor more proximally in the dorsal radius. A knotted 2- 0 two-strand braided suture is then placed in the dorsal lunate hole, which is followed by a 3 by 8 mm interference device to secure the graft and suture. This prevents loosening of the “chain” of reconstructed ligaments, while providing an anchor for the DIC (and DRC) ligament reconstruction. During closure, the DIC and DRC are imbricated to their insertion sites on the dorsal lunate using the previously placed 2-0 braided suture. In patients with SLAC I arthritic changes at the styloid tip, a 4 mm radial styloidectomy can be performed at the discretion of the surgeon, taking care to preserve the RSC ligament origin. The wounds are closed and the patient immobilized for 6 weeks in a below- elbow cast. ROM exercises are initiated at 6 weeks, and strengthening is gradually introduced at 12 weeks, with unrestricted activity at 6 months. The first 10 patients treated with this procedure were assessed at a minimum of 24 months postoperatively and demonstrated a median SL gap of 3 mm, SL angle of less than 70 degrees of all but one, maintenance of wrist ROM, and improvement in grip to 94% of the opposite side. One patient developed excessive flexion of the lunate and ulnocarpal impaction requiring secondary surgery. Longer- term clinical studies are needed to further validate this technique.

![[Fig.13.27.png]]
> **Fig. 13.27** A, The “window” approach to the carpus preserves all dorsal ligaments while yielding clear visualization of the carpus.131 Note the capsuloligamentous attachments of the DRC and DIC to the lunate. B, The addition of a midcarpal window yields visualization of the distal carpal row and the distal scaphoid, as well as inspection of the DIC and its underlying DST ligament subcomponent insertions on the proximal carpal row.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: ANAFAB Repair

Indications
- Complete SL rupture with diastasis
- Rotatory subluxation of the scaphoid
- Dorsal translation of the scaphoid
- Dorsal intercalated segment instability

Contraindications
- SLAC II or III arthritis
- Irreducible lunate extension
- Osteoporosis
- Arthroﬁbrosis

Preoperative Evaluation
- PA, true lateral of each wrist and pronated pencil grip
- MRI or arthroscopy to identify the status of SLIL, long RL, DRC, DST, and DIC
- MRI or arthroscopy to assess the integrity of the articular cartilage
- Flexion/extension lateral radiographs or dynamic ﬂuoroscopy to ensure reduc ibility

Technical Points
- Use an 8 cm dorsal incision, translocate EPL from third dorsal compartment.
- Excise a portion of the PIN at surgeon’s discretion.
- Use the radiocarpal “window” approach without altering DRC or DIC lunate attachments.
- Reduce and mark tunnels on scaphoid and lunate.
- Use a 10 cm volar “hockey- stick” incision over scaphoid tubercle.
- Anchor double strand of synthetic tape in trapezium.
- Expose volar carpus below FCR and identify long RL origin on radius.
- Drill guidewire followed by cannulated 3.0 mm radial tunnel from long RL origin to the Lister tubercle guided by ﬂuoroscopy.
- Drill guidewire followed by cannulated 3.0 mm scaphoid tunnel antegrade from dorsal approach to distal tubercle.
- Use rongeur to create a trough for the dSLIL graft.
```

**Stage 6: scapholunate dissociation with irreducible carpal collapse and normal cartilage.** This stage is defined by the presence of carpal malalignment that cannot be reduced easily owing to joint subluxation, fibrosis, and capsular contracture. By definition, if the carpus cannot be reduced easily, soft tissue reconstructions will fail and as such should be avoided.

If most of the cartilage surfaces are normal, symptomatic patients with impairment of occupational, recreational, or household tasks may be successfully treated with a proximal row carpectomy or a partial carpal fusion. Compared with a total wrist fusion, the advantages of only fusing the destroyed joint have long been recognized. What follows is a brief description of proximal row carpectomy and the most commonly used partial fusions for the treatment of SLD. Technical details and outcomes of these procedures are provided in Chapter 12.

Proximal row carpectomy. This is a salvage operation in which the scaphoid, lunate, and triquetrum are excised, creating a neoarticulation between the capitate and lunate fossa of the radius. In most published series, this procedure is associated with high overall patient satisfaction

With lunate manually reduced, drill guidewire followed by 3.0 mm cannulated lunate tunnel from just dorsal and at proximal margin of dSLIL origin to volar- ulnar pole of lunate, tangent to distal articular surface. Protect carpal tunnel contents. Harvest 15 cm by 2.5 mm strip of FCR tendon based distally, and combine it with synthetic tapes. Pass composite through scaphoid tunnel and apply ﬁrm tension. Insert 3.0 mm interference screw at scaphoid tubercle to ﬁx STT tension. Pass composite through lunate tunnel and retrieve through volar incision. Pass composite through radial tunnel. With maximum tension at the Lister tubercle, insert 3 mm interference screw palmar or dorsal to ﬁx long RL tension. Insert synthetic tape into a proximal 3.5 mm hole in radius and anchor it with a bone anchor to lock full tension in the repair. Fluoroscopic views to conﬁrm reduction. Insert knotted double-strand 2- 0 nonabsorbable suture in lunate tunnel dorsally. Insert 3 mm interference screw in dorsal lunate tunnel to ﬁx dSLIL tension and anchor dorsal suture. Imbricate DIC and DRC to dorsal lunate with anchored 2- 0 suture. Close window capsulotomy with running suture. No Kirschner wires needed. Skin closure and short- arm thumb spica splint.

Sutures out at 10 days and convert to short- arm thumb spica cast for 6 weeks. Start therapy for ROM all planes, including DTM. Start gentle strengthening at 3 months. Full return to activities at 6 months postoperative.

A single cohort series of 10 patients, minimum 24 m outcomes: SL gap 3 mm, improvements in all SL angles to normal range in 9 of 10, improved grip strength of 94% contralateral, maintenance of wrist ROM. Longer- term clinical studies are needed to validate this technique.

in terms of pain relief, restoration of functional wrist motion, and grip strength.

In mechanical terms, this operation converts a complex and highly evolved articulation into a single joint with nonmatching articular surfaces. The wrist can adjust to such an incongruity in the short to medium term, only if there is a good articular cartilage on the proximal pole of the capitate and on the lunate fossa of the radius. Compared with midcarpal arthrodesis, immobilization times are generally shorter and it eliminates the risk of nonunion. It may be converted into a wrist arthrodesis or arthroplasty at a later date should it degenerate. Long- term radiocapitate degeneration, although often asymptomatic, is present in at least a third of patients with more than 10 years of follow- up. In one series, 6 out of 17 patients at 20 years’ follow- up had been reoperated on. With a success rate of 65%, Wall et al. recommends preoperative counseling when this procedure is offered to patients younger than 35 years old. Wagner et al. compared the longterm (average follow- up of 11 years) outcome of four- corner fusion to proximal row carpectomy in 89 patients under 45 years of age and noted that both procedures had similar outcomes and radiographic arthritis, with those in the latter group having improved motion and fewer complications.

Scaphoid–trapezium–trapezoid arthrodesis. The goal of this procedure is to reduce the proximal pole of the scaphoid into the scaphoid fossa, restoring RS congruency and decreasing the chance of developing degenerative changes. When planning, it is important that the external dimensions and alignment of the resultant fused block are the same as the external dimensions of the bones in the normal wrist. Underreduction (scaphoid flexed) fails to close the SL gap correctly, and overreduction (scaphoid extended) may result in more restricted motion and more severe RS impingement postoperatively.

As experience with STT joint fusion has increased, complications and long- term outcomes have been reported. The average rate of nonunion among all the reported series is 14%. Postoperatively, ROM and strength are frequently decreased, and the results in terms of pain relief are not uniformly predictable. Painful RS impingement is a frequent problem, owing to the fact that in radial deviation the distally fused scaphoid can no longer rotate into flexion, resulting in increased pressure on the scaphoid fossa. To solve this problem, Rogers and Watson recommended incorporating a dorsolateral radial styloidectomy as part of the STT joint fusion. Any preexisting cartilage degeneration between the scaphoid and radius is a formal contraindication to STT joint fusion.

Scapho- lunate arthrodesis. In a cadaveric analysis, Han et al. examined the changes in wrist motion after simulated SL arthrodesis and found that fusion only decreased wrist flexion and DT motion by 9 degrees or less. Fusion is conceptually an ideal solution for SLD; however, SL fusion has proved to be one of the least reliable treatments for this condition. The long moment arms of the scaphoid and the intercalated segment on the small surface area of the SL articulation, combined with the distraction vector produced by the loaded capitate head make this fusion difficult to achieve. Following arthrodesis, the normal rotation and translation that occurs between the two bones is no longer possible, resulting in increased stress on the articulation, and predisposing the fusion site to fracture. Bony union occurs in only 50% of cases. Maintaining the geometry of the articulating capitate scaphoid lunate joint is critical to prevent altered joint contact forces and degenerative change.

Scaphoid–capitate arthrodesis. Theoretically, SC fusion should have consequences similar to STT joint fusion. In the laboratory, the kinematic and kinetic carpal behavior after both types of fusion are similar—that is, both cause abnormal transfer of load and significant loss of midcarpal joint motion, especially in radial and ulnar deviation. Watson and coworkers found less of a reduction of motion after STT joint fusion than after SC fusion, probably because of increased rotation at the trapezoid- capitate joint. The long- term results of this procedure in one series are quite acceptable, with more than two- thirds of patients satisfied, with minimal disability, although approximately 30% of patients develop degenerative changes at the RS joint.

**Stage 7: scapholunate dissociation with irreducible carpal collapse and degenerative arthritis.** Chronic carpal collapse, with irreducible subluxation of the scaphoid, will cause radiocarpal and midcarpal cartilage loss. This occurs in a predictable and progressive pattern known as scapholunate advanced collapse (SLAC) (see page 509).

SLAC arthritis is not likely to be alleviated with soft tissue procedures. The following are some points to be considered when using these techniques in patients with SLD. Detailed information about these procedures is provided in Chapters 12, 16, and 17.

Arthroscopy. Wrist arthroscopy can be used to evaluate the degree and extent of articular degeneration to decide which salvage procedure is best. It can be a valuable adjunct in determining the status of the radial and capitate articular cartilages, particularly in the decisionmaking process between a four- corner fusion or proximal row carpectomy. There may be a limited therapeutic benefit to debridement and synovectomy. Arthroscopy can also be utilized for soft tissue interposition procedures and partial wrist arthrodesis. Further details of the role of wrist arthroscopy in the treatment of SLAC wrist can be found in Chapter 17.

Radial styloidectomy. This is one of the oldest procedures used to relieve wrist pain caused by an impingement between the tip of the radial styloid and a malaligned scaphoid. Despite removing the degenerated radial styloid process, the SLAC wrist sequence will continue to progress. When performing a radial styloidectomy (RS) through an open lateral approach, caution is required to protect the dorsolateral branches of the superficial radial nerve. At its palmar margin, it is important to avoid detaching the origin of the radiocarpal ligaments, which would lead to iatrogenic instability. For individuals who desire minimal surgical intervention, arthroscopic radial styloidectomy may be an option (see Chapter 17). The procedure may be combined with partial wrist denervation (below).

Partial wrist denervation. Partial wrist denervation of the terminal branches of the anterior interosseous nerve (AIN) and posterior interosseous nerve (PIN) offers the advantage of improved pain relief with minimal recovery time without burning bridges for other wrist salvage procedures. Weinstein and Berger reported on 19 patients with chronic wrist pain over a 5 year period and at an average of 2.5 years after surgery; 80% reported improved pain, 73% of patients returned to work, and 45% had normal or increased grip strength. O’Shaughnessy and colleagues noted that at an average of 6.75 years after isolated partial wrist denervation, 69% of patients didn’t require further surgery. Less reliable outcomes were noted in patients who had multiple previous surgeries or those with advanced posttraumatic/SLAC arthritis.

Radioscaphoid–lunate fusion and distal scaphoidectomy. Many daily occupational and recreational activities involve motion along the dart- thrower’s plane, from radial- extension to ulnarflexion. This motion occurs primarily at the midcarpal joint.Consequently, if the cartilage of the midcarpal joint is normal, and fixed malalignment or degenerative arthritis of the scaphoid and lunate requires an arthrodesis, it is reasonable to fuse the RSL joint. Fusing it eliminates pain induced by degenerative arthritis, while stabilizing the “acetabulum” of the midcarpal joint.

Fixation of the scaphoid to the radius severely constrains normal motion at the STT and SC joints. To “unlock” the midcarpal joint, Garcia- Elias et al. suggested excision of the distal third of the scaphoid (Fig. 13.41). Pervaiz et al. demonstrated further increases in motion in a cadaveric model of RSL fusion when excising the triquetrum.With these modifications, the midcarpal ball- and- socket articulation is freed from its mediolateral constraints, allowing more than 50% of normal wrist motion. Furthermore, the chances of achieving an RSL arthrodesis are improved due to the reduction in cantilever loads on the distal scaphoid. From a surgical perspective, the chances of a durable outcome are improved if the normal relationship of the scaphoid and lunate is restored so that an anatomic articulation is maintained with the distal carpal row.

![[Fig.13.41.png]]
> **Fig. 13.41** Schematic representation (A) and case example (B) of a radioscapholunate fusion with distal scaphoidectomy: an option when a scapholunate dissociation is not easily reducible and the cartilage at the midcarpal joint is intact. The technique preserves the important midcarpal joint and the dart-thrower’s motion. Distal scaphoid excision allows increased radioulnar deviation, and the triquetrum can be excised at the surgeon’s discretion.

Scaphoidectomy and midcarpal fusion. Popularized by Watson and coworkers, the SLAC procedure (i.e., scaphoid excision plus lunate–capitate–triquetrum–hamate fusion, also known as four- corner fusion) has been used for the treatment of chronic SLD. Traverso et al. reported that functional results and patient satisfaction rates were high at a minimum follow- up of 10 years, despite a 73% radiographic rate of degenerative changes within the RL joint. For the operation to be successful, excellent articular cartilage at the RL level is required. If the lunate is not fully reduced, a frequent complication is the development of dorsal impingement between the dorsal edge of the radius and the capitate. Several implants, including circular or square plates, staples, and locking screws, have been designed to be countersunk below the dorsal intersection of the four carpal bones so that dorsal impingement is avoided.

Some authors prefer to limit fusion to the LC joint, and reported results have been comparable. In patients with ulnar- positive variance, or in cases with chronic combined SL and LT instability, the scaphoid and triquetrum can be excised before fusing the LC joint. In a biomechanical study, excision of the scaphoid and triquetrum has been shown to increase radioulnar deviation by 7 degrees and flexionextension arc by 6 degrees. Cohen et al. noted that scaphoid excision and four-bone fusion increases the load to the RL joint which is further exacerbated when the triquetrum is excised.

Total wrist arthroplasty. Advances in total joint replacement of the wrist have made this option a reasonable treatment for selected osteoarthritic patients. Most patients with late posttraumatic instability are young and active individuals or manual laborers, for whom a total joint prosthesis is seldom an acceptable choice (see Chapter 12 for more details). If the cartilage of the proximal surface of the capitate is preserved, there is an option of replacing only the proximal row by a hemiarthroplasty. Anneberg et al. reviewed a series of 20 patients at an average of 4 years after a radial component hemiarthroplasty and demonstrated improved wrist motion, grip strength, and outcome scores. Three patients required a manipulation under anesthesia in the early postoperative period for stiffness. One patient underwent internal fixation of the fourth CMC joint following a fall. One patient ultimately required wrist arthrodesis for progression of arthritis, and two hemiarthroplasties were converted to total wrist arthroplasty.

Total wrist arthrodesis. Although many surgeons believe that a total wrist arthrodesis should rarely be considered in patients with posttraumatic osteoarthritis, arthrodesis may still be preferred in patients who perform heavy manual work (see Chapter 12). According to some sources, complete pain relief can be expected in 85% of patients, with 65% of them returning to their former occupations. As shown in many clinical series, most patients with total wrist fusion are able to accomplish all daily tasks by learning to compensate for the loss of wrist motion.

### AUTHORS' PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: SL DISSOCIATION

Symptomatic partial ruptures of the SL ligamentous complex (i.e., Garcia- Elias stage 1, occult SLD), if diagnosed within a few weeks of injury, generally respond favorably to pin or temporary screw fixation and casting for 6 to 8 weeks. Subacute and chronic injuries may respond well to a period of immobilization followed by dedicated hand therapy. Particularly promising are the results of proprioception reeducation of the wrist muscular stabilizers, promoting the combined action of the APL and ECRL/ECRB and FCU muscles. Failing nonoperative treatment or in more advanced instances of SL injury, we prefer highresolution MRI or diagnostic wrist arthroscopy to define the nature and location of the injury, to examine the articular cartilage, and to assess the integrity of the critical ligament stabilizers of the proximal row.

Arthroscopic electrothermal shrinkage or debridement and pinning of the injured or stretched proximal membrane is a promising option in early Geissler grades. It is always important to check for the integrity of the dSLIL. If it is insufficient or torn but repairable (stage 2, SLD), one can perform the arthroscopic DCSS capsuloplasty procedure as described by Mathoulin et al. or an open ligament repair, with particular attention to repair any element of DIC and DRC avulsion to the bare area of the lunate with bone anchors.

Although recognizing its potential use, we no longer use bone–ligament–bone grafts in stage 3 SLD. In our opinion, isolated reconstruction of the dSLIL is not indicated in more advanced stages where the scaphoid’s proximal and distal stabilizers are lost and the scaphoid has already yielded into flexion and pronation. If there is sufficient dSLIL to repair, a dorsal capsulodesis should be performed to augment the repair by counteracting scaphoid malrotation. We no longer consider dSLIL ligament repair, even if reparable, when there is substantial lunate malalignment. Indeed, the presence of DISI indicates a more global problem that can hardly be solved by addressing only the dSLIL.

If scaphoid subluxation is easily correctable (stage 4, SLD), lunate posture is maintained, and no cartilage defect exists, we prefer a tendon graft procedure (with or without synthetic augmentation), as these procedures address the inevitably injured critical stabilizers. There are no comparative clinical studies of the many different techniques, but the authors prefer a technique that addresses both the dorsal and volar components of this injury, such as the box repairs or SL360, the ANAFAB, the TLT, or the SLT reconstructions. If the lunate is in DISI (stage 5, SLD), we assess for reducibility. If DISI is reducible, one of us prefers the ANAFAB technique to reconstruct the dorsal and volar critical stabilizers and reduce lunate position. The other has early favorable experience with the SL 360 technique and the ECRL modification of the spiral tenodesis as well. We acknowledge, however, that at the time of this writing, no technique has a long- term proven track record of maintaining reduction of DISI or ulnar translation.

In the rare case that malalignment is irreducible (stage 6, SLD) and normal midcarpal cartilage is confirmed by arthroscopy or MRI, we have achieved good results by either proximal row carpectomy or fusing the radiocarpal joint (RSL fusion) and excising the distal third of the scaphoid (see Fig. 13.41). This is a technically demanding procedure, with no tolerance for incongruity of the midcarpal joint. More commonly, when the RS joint is involved by the arthritic process (stage 7, SLD, SLAC wrist), we consider a partial wrist denervation with radial styloidectomy in early stages as a limited intervention that can preserve motion and function. Success can generally be predicted by a positive response to a lidocaine/marcaine block in the office. We will offer a proximal row carpectomy in an older or less-active population, provided capitate and lunate facet cartilage is intact. Alternatively, in a younger or heavy demand patient, especially when the LC joint is arthritic and the lunate reducible, we prefer a scaphoid excision and four- corner fusion. Newer designs of total wrist arthroplasty may play an increasing role in the treatment of SLAC wrist if problems of component loosening in the younger, active population can be overcome (see Chapter 12).

![[Fig.13.41.png]]
> **Fig. 13.41** Schematic representation (A) and case example (B) of a radioscapholunate fusion with distal scaphoidectomy: an option when a scapholunate dissociation is not easily reducible and the cartilage at the midcarpal joint is intact. The technique preserves the important midcarpal joint and the dart-thrower’s motion. Distal scaphoid excision allows increased radioulnar deviation, and the triquetrum can be excised at the surgeon’s discretion.

### Lunotriquetral Dissociation

Posttraumatic or degenerative LT dissociations are not unusual, though the literature may be scarce and often misleading. The first case of a LT dissociation was identified in the early 20th century, but it was not until the 1970s, following the work of Reagan and associates, that the problem became more widely recognized. Since then, this problem is better understood, and treatment results have improved. Yet the chance that this condition is missed or confused with other ulnar- sided wrist problems (e.g., midcarpal instabilities or TFCC injuries) remains high and as such, the practitioner should have a systematic approach for managing ulnar- sided wrist pain.

#### Pathomechanics of LT Dissociation

Most isolated injuries to the LT ligaments are secondary to a backward fall onto the outstretched hand, with the arm externally rotated, the forearm supinated, and the wrist extended and radially inclined. In such circumstances, the impact is concentrated on the hypothenar area, and particularly on the pisiform, which acts as a punch against the extended triquetrum. Such a dorsally directed force to the triquetrum induces its dorsal displacement. The lunate does not follow the triquetrum because it is constrained by the radius and the long and short RL ligaments. As a result of this, substantial shear stress is created at the LT joint, causing progressive stretching and ultimately tearing of the three components of the LT ligaments (i.e., volar, dorsal, and proximal). If, aside from this, there is a sudden rotation of the distal row into further pronation, the stout THC ligament further increases the applied moment on the palmar LT ligament and rupture ensues. Supporting this explanation is that VISI is seldom seen acutely in these injuries, suggesting that the palmar LT and THC ligaments are rarely disrupted simultaneously.

Injury to the LT ligaments is often associated with peripheral tears of the TFCC and a distal avulsion of the UT ligament. The mechanism of these combined injuries is similar to the mechanism discussed for the isolated LT injury, except for the predominance of radial inclination and pronation as torque- inducing vectors. In this respect, any ligament avulsion of the palmar rim of the triquetrum should be interpreted as a potential sign of a combined ulnocarpal and LT injury.

In other cases, a perilunate destabilization process, as postulated by Mayfield and colleagues, may lead to LT instability. In such instances, the wrist has undergone sudden extension, ulnar inclination, and midcarpal supination, usually from motorcycle accidents or falls from a height. Rupture of the SL ligaments (stage I) occurs first, followed by LC dislocation (stage II), and finally rupture of the LT ligaments (stage III). If the SL ligament lesion is successfully treated but the LT lesion remains, symptoms from LT instability may require treatment.

The LT joint may become progressively disrupted as the result of a long- standing ulnocarpal abutment or impaction. In the presence of an ulnar- positive variant, attritional degeneration of the proximal membranous portion of the LT joint is frequent and should never be confused or treated as if it was the result of an isolated traumatic event.

In the laboratory, several attempts to ascertain the consequences of LT ligament disruptions have been made. When the LT and DRCs were experimentally sectioned in axially loaded cadaveric wrists, the flexion moment of the scaphoid became unconstrained, causing a conjoint flexion of the scaphoid and lunate, with secondary anterior subluxation of the capitate (Fig. 13.42). This represents the more advanced stage of the disease: a static VISI pattern of instability. By contrast, when only the palmar and dorsal LT ligaments were sectioned, increased mobility of the LT joint was detected (dynamic instability) but not a complete destabilization of the carpus. These findings may explain the relatively low incidence of radiocarpal arthrosis in late static LT dissociations. This is probably because the scaphoid and lunate maintain their normal alignment with the radius.

![[Fig.13.42.png]]
> **Fig. 13.42** A, Schematic representation of typical carpal bone displacements (curved arrows) that occur when the extrinsic and intrinsic lunotriquetral-supporting ligaments have failed; the unconstrained triquetrum migrates proximally, especially in ulnar inclination (straight arrows); and the distal row supinates. B, In this radiograph of LT dissociation, the foreshortened scaphoid and triangular-shaped lunate indicates their rotation into flexion.

#### Diagnosis of LT Dissociation

LT dissociation may present as a spectrum of clinical conditions, ranging from an asymptomatic partial tear to a painful complete dissociation with static VISI collapse. Although the former does not deform the wrist, the latter may exhibit a reversed “fork- like” or “bayonet” deformity. This is similar to the deformity seen in volarly displaced distal radius fractures, except that the carpal sag is distal and not lateral to the prominent distal ulna. Some patients describe painful crepitus or carpal shifting as they ulnarly deviate their hand.Symptomatic injuries invariably exhibit point tenderness directly over the dorsal aspect of the joint. Pain is usually aggravated with ulnar deviation of the wrist and supination of the forearm. Wrist motion is seldom diminished except in the more advanced cases with carpal collapse. Patients frequently complain of weakness and a sensation of the wrist giving way, and some may also have ulnar nerve paresthesias.

#### Physical Examination

A pathognomonic finding is a positive ballottement test, as described by Reagan and coworkers. The lunate is firmly stabilized with the thumb and index finger of one hand, while the triquetrum and pisiform are displaced dorsally and palmarly with the other hand. A positive result elicits pain, occasionally crepitus, and abnormal displacement of the joint. The so- called shear test described by Kleinman is a variation of the ballottement test: By stabilizing the dorsal aspect of the lunate with the thumb of one hand, the pisiform is loaded by the examiner’s other thumb in a dorsal direction, creating a shear force at the LT joint that causes pain (Fig. 13.43).

![[Fig.13.43.png]]
> **Fig. 13.43** A, The “shear test” as described by Kleinman197 is similar to the ballottement test described by Reagan and coworkers.194 B, Opposite forces (black arrows) are applied to induce abnormal displacement (red arrows) to a lunotriquetral joint suspected of ligament injury. This test is painful for a patient with a sprain or instability of the LT joint.

The Derby test reported by Christodoulou and Bainbridge is also useful in the LT dissociation with a reducible VISI type of deformity.This test starts by realigning the lunate and scaphoid relative to the radius. This is done by placing the wrist in extension and at a slight ulnar deviation. If, in that position, we reduce the LT joint by pushing the pisiform dorsally, the feeling of instability disappears immediately, and grip strength increases as long as pressure over the pisiform is maintained.

Most of these provocative maneuvers are reasonably sensitive but not specific. Painful ulnar- sided conditions that may elicit a pain from these tests include LT synchondrosis, fracture of an incomplete congenital coalition; triquetrum- hamate impingement and degeneration; avulsion fractures of the dorsal triquetrum; pisotriquetral arthropathy; traumatic or degenerative TFCC tears; ulnocarpal impaction syndrome; ulnar styloid impingement, ECU tenosynovitis; or entrapment of the dorsal branch of the ulnar nerve. Indeed, careful differential diagnosis is mandatory before deciding on a treatment for ulnar- sided pain.

#### Radiographic Examination

Standard wrist radiographs appear normal in most patients with partial tears of the LT interosseous membrane. Occasionally, in chronic dynamic instabilities, a slight narrowing of the LT joint with subchondral cyst formation on the opposing sides of the joint may appear. This finding can be confused with similar features seen in patients with a fracture of an incomplete LT coalition.

When there is a static VISI pattern of malalignment secondary to complete rupture of both intrinsic and extrinsic LT supporting ligaments, radiographic diagnosis is obvious. Most characteristic is the disruption of the normal convex arc of the proximal carpal row (Gilula’s line) in the PA radiograph. A “moon- like” lunate facing a foreshortened scaphoid is also typical of this condition (Fig. 13.44A; see also Fig. 13.16B). In such cases, the lunate’s dorsal pole is superimposed on the proximal pole of the capitate, implying an abnormal flexion of the bone. Additionally, the pathognomonic “seagull sign” is created on the PA view when the distal outlines of the lunate and triquetrum adopt the form of a flying seagull (see Fig. 13.44B). A paradoxically widened SL joint is common in LT dissociations. It is important to note, however, that the gap does not represent a rupture of the SL ligaments but is instead the result of an axial load being applied to the most volar SL ligament fibers. These fibers are longer and obliquely oriented, allowing an increased but normal separation of the two bones. None of these radiographic findings change with the wrist in ulnar deviation, except for an increased proximal displacement of the triquetrum relative to the rest of the proximal carpal row (Fig. 13.45). In collapsed cases, the carpal height ratio may be diminished.

![[Fig.13.44.png]]
> **Fig. 13.44** A, Posteroanterior view of a static lunotriquetral dissociation in a 44-year-old lawyer who had suffered a fall from a height 2 years before, without apparent consequences. Six months after the accident, he complained of painful snapping during lateral inclinations. Note the moon-like configuration of the lunate in volar intercalated segmental instability. B, The pathognomonic “seagull”- appearing outline of the distal LT border (red line).

![[Fig.13.45.png]]
> **Fig. 13.45** Dynamic radiographs of static lunotriquetral dissociation. A, During radial deviation, the scaphoid and the lunate are abnormally palmar flexed, but the triquetrum is normally aligned relative to the lunate (up arrow). B, During ulnar deviation, there is a distal step-off between the lunate and the triquetrum (down arrow). Note the scaphoid and lunate remain in the flexed position, despite the ulnar deviation of the wrist.

![[Fig.13.16.png]]
> **Fig. 13.16** The shape of the lunate on a posteroanterior view may help differentiate a dislocated from a malaligned lunate. A, The lunate in a dorsal intercalated segment instability (DISI) position tends to have an ovoid configuration, with a prominent ulnar corner pointing toward the medial aspect of the wrist. B, The lunate in volar intercalated segment instability (VISI) has a “C”- shaped or moon-like appearance. C, In dorsal perilunate dislocations, the subluxed lunate appears as an isosceles triangle pointing distally.

In the lateral projection, aside from the obvious VISI posture, it is not uncommon to find a decreased LT angle. According to Reagan et al., the average LT angle is 14 degrees. Assessment of this angular measurement requires radiographs of excellent quality and considerable practice interpreting them because the longitudinal axis of the triquetrum is difficult to identify. Yet identifying the relative angle of the triquetral axis relative to the lunate axis may help discriminate between a CIND- VISI and an LT dissociation (Fig. 13.46). In CIND- VISI wrists, the triquetral axis is angled volar in relation to the lunate axis. In the LT dissociated wrist, by contrast, the triquetral axis is angled dorsal to the lunate’s axis.

![[Fig.13.46.png]]
> **Fig. 13.46** A, The lunotriquetral angle is difficult to identify in a lateral radiograph of a normal wrist, and its quantification difficult to reproduce. Yet identifying the relative angle of the triquetral (Tq) axis relative to the lunate axis may be of help to discriminate between a (B) carpal instability nondissociative (CIND-VISI) wrist and a wrist with (C) LT dissociation. Both in normal wrists and in CIND-VISI wrists, the triquetral axis is angled volar to the lunate’s axis. In the LT dissociated wrist, by contrast, the triquetral axis is always angled dorsal to the lunate’s longitudinal axis.

#### Other Diagnostic Tests

Advanced imaging is particularly useful when there is doubt about the etiology of a chronic LT tear. If the injury is due to a chronic ulnocarpal impingement, MRI frequently shows an area of increased signal in the lunate, accompanied by central perforation of the triangular fibrocartilage disc. High- resolution noncontrast MRI using at least a 1.5 T magnet and a dedicated wrist coil provides detailed information concerning the integrity of the LT ligament subsections, as well as the supporting intrinsic and extrinsic ligaments of the LT complex.

Arthrography is rarely helpful and may be misleading in the diagnosis of ulnar- sided wrist pain. An arthrographic communication through the LT joint is nonspecific and may result from a traumatic injury, a chronic age- related perforation, or ligament degeneration caused by ulnocarpal impaction syndrome. Plain lateral radiographs in ulnar and radial deviation or dynamic fluoroscopy are inexpensive and a helpful tests for VISI deformities. In relatively acute LT dissociations with VISI, the wrist may still exhibit a sudden reduction of the abnormally flexed lunate during ulnar deviation, manifesting as a clunk. However, such self- reducing capability is rapidly lost, and VISI may become fixed. In such instances, as the wrist moves from radialto- ulnar deviation, the triquetral- hamate joint is always engaged, and the SL complex remains in the flexed position in ulnar inclination.

Arthroscopy has become an increasingly important diagnostic and therapeutic tool for the evaluation of lesions on the ulnar side of the carpus. It allows the surgeon to confirm the location of the ligament injury (dorsal, membranous, volar, or complete), the reducibility of the dissociation, the status of the cartilage, and associated pathology such as ulnar impaction or hamate arthrosis secondary to a type II lunate. Further technical details are provided in Chapter 17.

#### Treatment: Lunotriquetral Dissociation

As with all other forms of carpal instability, treatment of LT instability should be individualized according to the patient’s age, occupation, recreational demands, and intensity of symptoms. LT injuries may be acute or chronic. They may involve only the LT interosseous ligaments and maintain normal bony alignment, or they may have a more global intrinsic and extrinsic ligament insufficiency resulting in carpal collapse. An injury may appear as an isolated ulnar- sided problem or be part of a perilunate instability. There are different clinical conditions in which there is substantial damage to the LT supporting ligaments, each having its own prognosis and treatment. In general, LTIL ruptures, unlike SLIL ruptures, are not necessarily a prearthritic condition. A description of the treatment of the three most common forms of LT ligament injury seen in clinical practice follows. Perilunate injuries involving the SL and LT joints will be discussed in a later section (page 545). Cases in which there is degeneration of the LT proximal membrane as a result of a chronic ulnocarpal abutment are not included in this section (see Chapter 14).

**Clinical forms of LT dissociation.** Several classification systems have been proposed that describe the injury based on its chronicity, location of injury, and position of the carpus. Viegas and colleaguesconducted an anatomic and biomechanical study and proposed the following classification:
- Stage 1: Partial or complete disruption of the LT interosseous ligament, without clinical and/or radiographic evidence of dynamic or static VISI
- Stage 2: Complete disruption of LT interosseous and palmar lig aments, with clinical and/or radiographic evidence of reducible VISI deformity aments, attenuation, or disruption of the DRC, with clinical and radiographic evidence of static VISI deformity

Stage 1: acute LT injury without carpal collapse. The so- called dynamic or occult LT instability is usually diagnosed by arthroscopy. The degree of LT ligament incompetence varies from a minimal disruption to complete rupture of the proximal membrane and both volar and dorsal LT ligaments; yet, the proximal row does not exhibit abnormal posture because the LT supporting ligaments are not injured. The condition may be painful as a result of increased motion between the two bones, generating shear stress and local synovitis.

- Stage 3: Complete disruption of LT interosseous and palmar lig

Most authors agree that acute LT injuries should be treated conservatively, with the wrist immobilized in a carefully molded cast or splint, with a pad beneath the pisiform and over the dorsum of the distal radius to augment immobilization of the LT joint. Reagan et al. noted that 6 of 7 patients treated acutely in a cast or splint had improvement of their symptoms. Failures using this approach can occur. Some authors recommend that the cast be extended above the elbow to prevent motion at the LT joint during forearm rotation, owing to the pistoning of the ulna against the carpus through the TFCC.

The introduction of arthroscopy in wrist trauma allowed earlier recognition of the extent of these lesions and an increased awareness of their potential risk. To increase the rigidity of immobilization, some authors recommend arthroscopically assisted percutaneous pinning of the acutely injured LT joint for 8 to 10 weeks (see Chapter 17). Osterman and Seidman published an 80% success rate with this treatment strategy and emphasized the importance of confirmation of reduction by midcarpal arthroscopic inspection. Weiss et al. reported on the outcomes of arthroscopic debridement of LT injury and noted 100% improvement in partial and 78% in complete injuries.

Should nonoperative or percutaneous treatment fail, open repair of complete injuries of the ligament has been described, using transosseous drill holes or suture anchors, through a dorsal, volar, or combined approach, though over 75% had complications or reoperation at 5 years. The LT joint was stabilized by two Kirschner wires, following which the ligament was repaired to the site of its avulsion. If a dorsal approach was performed, the repair was further reinforced using a dorsal capsulodesis. If performing open repair, it is important to remember the positive stabilizing effects of ECU muscle proprioceptive reeducation to provide a pronation moment to the distal carpal row, which may help prevent VISI.

Stage 2: chronic LT injury without carpal collapse. LT instability is considered chronic when the two ends of the disrupted ligaments have degenerated, diminishing the chances for successful healing. If the intrinsic and extrinsic supporting ligaments are intact, carpal alignment may remain normal. Reagan and colleagues noted that cast treatment improved symptoms in only 1 of 4 patients with chronic LT injury. In symptomatic cases, a more aggressive approach is necessary to reestablish the synchronicity of motion between the triquetrum and lunate.

A variety of strategies have been proposed, including simple arthroscopic debridement, electrothermal shrinkage, ligament reconstruction using a strip of the ECU tendon, and LT arthrodesis.None of these methods have attained consistent success for chronic injuries.

The various arthroscopic techniques for the management of chronic tears of the LT interosseous ligament are reviewed in Chapter 17. These include debridement of the frayed ends of the disrupted proximal membrane, capsular shrinkage, or closing the interval between the UL and ulnotriquetrum ligaments with sutures using arthroscopic visualization.

Open repair can be performed if the quality of the ligament is suitable and can be augmented by a capsulodesis, as needed. Omokawa et al. treated 11 patients with Kirschner wire stabilization of the LT joint, primary repair of the ligament using suture anchors and DRC capsulodesis.
