After a mean follow- up of 31 months, outcomes were promising with an improvement of grip strength, pain, and Mayo wrist scores.

In a study comparing repair, arthrodesis, and tendon graft reconstruction of reducible chronic instability due to complete disruption of the LT ligaments, Shin et al. demonstrated that ligament reconstruction with a tendon graft was the most reliable technique (Fig. 13.47). The technique consists of reconstructing the LT linkage with a strip of the ECU tendon, left attached distally and passed through holes in the lunate and triquetrum. By tightly looping the tendon graft around the LT joint, immediate stability is achieved. The reconstruction is further secured by transfixing the joint with one or two Kirschner wires for 8 weeks, followed by 4 more weeks in a protective splint. A group of 8 patients treated with this technique showed encouraging results.

![[Fig.13.47.png]]
> **Fig. 13.47** A 44-year-old policeman who was injured in a fight had painful chronic dynamic lunotriquetral dissociation. A complete nonrepairable LT injury was found arthroscopically (A), so it was decided to perform an open tendon reconstruction of the palmar (B) and dorsal (C) LT ligaments using a slip of the extensor carpi ulnaris tendon, according to Shin and colleagues.201 The patient had regained normal hand function 6 months after surgery with 35% reduced flexion but a strong and painless grip.

Shahane and coworkers published a different approach, also using a strip of ECU tendon in 46 patients. A distally based strip of the ECU was passed through two drill holes in the triquetrum, posterior aspect of the triangular fibrocartilaginous complex and capsule of the posterior DRUJ and imbricated to itself. After an average follow- up of over 3 years, good to excellent results were noted in 29 patients, satisfactory in 11 and poor in 6. Five of the 6 complications pertained to the pisotriquetral joint.

Fusing the unstable LT joint, arthrodesis is another alternative. The procedure has met with variable success and a high nonunion rate. According to Guidera et al., most complications of LT fusion result from technical problems. By using cancellous bone graft to fill a biconcave space created in the adjoining bones and stabilizing the joint with multiple Kirschner wires, these authors reported a 100% consolidation rate. In their series of 26 procedures, postoperative flexion–extension averaged 78% of the range measured on the contralateral side, with good or excellent pain relief in 83% of the cases and with 88% of patients returning to their previous occupations. These results including 100% fusion rate have not been replicated in the literature. A metaanalysis of 143 LT fusions concluded that LT fusions are not without technical problems and complications, demonstrating a nonunion rate of 26% and a complication rate of 43%, mostly in the form of persistent pain. Nelson and colleagues noted in 22 patients undergoing LT fusion, union rates were greater in patients who were treated with a combination of compression screw and Kirschner wire fixation (91% union rate) compared with Kirschner wire alone (60% fused), and advocated for immobilization greater than 6 weeks. The procedure should not be performed in the presence of a VISI deformity, as fusion will only convert a dissociative VISI into a nondissociative VISI.

The only study comparing ligament reconstruction with LT fusion, as noted above, has been reported by Shin and colleagues. They retrospectively reviewed 57 patients with an average postoperative follow- up of 9.5 years. Patients who underwent tendon reconstruction of the LT ligaments had higher subjective and objective outcomes and a lower complication rate than patients having an arthrodesis.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: LT Arthrodesis

Indications
- Dynamic LT instability secondary to complete intrinsic ligament rupture in the absence of an ulnocarpal abutment syndrome (i.e., normal TFCC) and normal midcarpal joint

Contraindications
- Radiocarpal or midcarpal arthritis
- Ulnar positive variance
- VISI

Preoperative Evaluation
- Positive LT ballottement test
- Fluoroscopic evaluation of abnormal proximal- distal shift of the triquetrum during radioulnar inclination
- Arthroscopy to rule out TFCC degeneration, ulnocarpal impaction, or triquetrum- hamate impingement

Technical Points
- Perform a dorsal (e.g., zigzag, lazy “S,” or longitudinal) incision centered at the IV- V septum.
- Perform a longitudinal incision of the extensor retinaculum along the V compartment.
- Open the septum between IV and V; coagulate intraseptal artery. Perform a PIN neurectomy if the nerve appears damaged or has a neuroma. Limited capsulotomy to expose the LT joint.
- Complete section of the remnants of LT ligaments.
- Open the LT as a book and remove the adjacent articular surfaces with a dental rongeur or high- speed bur with copious saline irrigation to expose cancellous bone. Do not excise the rim of the opposing cortical edges to preserve the normal intercarpal separation.
- Harvest cancellous bone from the radius.
- Two 0.062- inch nonparallel Kirschner wires are preset in the ulnar aspect of the triquetrum. Beware of the dorsal branch of the ulnar nerve.
- Bone graft is densely packed in the biconcave cavity.
- The joint is reduced, and the Kirschner wires are driven into the lunate and their position veriﬁed.
- One wire is used to insert a headless cannulated compression screw.
- Cut the second wire below the skin’s surface.
- Perform standard capsular and retinacular closure.

Postoperative Regimen
- Short- arm thumb spica cast for at least 6 weeks, changed at 10 days for stitch removal and radiographs
- At 6 weeks, cast removal, radiographs, and new cast applied
- Follow with out-of-plaster radiographs until healing is obvious, conﬁrm with CT scan, and remove Kirschner wire under local anesthesia.
- Rehabilitation after cast removal to regain motion and grip strength
- Contact sports not allowed until 6 months postoperatively.
- If successful: 75% of patients have a painless wrist with 80% of global motion and 75% grip strength.
- Caution: high rate of complications and nonunion
```

Stage 3: chronic LT dissociation with carpal collapse. LT dissociation with carpal collapse is secondary to complete disruption of all portions of the LT interosseous ligament and attenuation or disruption of the dorsal and palmar intrinsic and extrinsic stabilizing ligaments—predominantly the DRC and ulnar arcuate ligaments. As a result of such a global ligament insufficiency, the carpus collapses into a dissociative VISI pattern of malalignment. As the underlying pathology involves the intrinsic and extrinsic ligaments, fusion of the lunate and triquetrum is unsuccessful in reducing VISI. Neither an isolated fusion of the LT joint nor any sort of tendon reconstruction is likely to reliably correct such a complex unstable condition in maintaining control of the lunate. Halikis and associates recommended RL fusion for static dissociative VISI. Excision of the triquetrum could be considered in this situation and utilized as bone graft.

### AUTHORS' PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: LT DISSOCIATION

We believe that acute complete tears of the LT ligaments are best treated by above- elbow cast immobilization alone or with arthroscopically assisted percutaneous LT joint fixation with Kirschner wires. The potential for improved ligament healing by more rigid fixation may outweigh the potential morbidity of pinning the joint. Temporary cannulated screw fixation of the LT joint may enable earlier mobilization of the wrist. We are not aware of any comparative outcome studies of above- elbow casting versus temporary fixation for acute complete LT injuries.

For chronic instability without carpal collapse, we recommend ligament reconstruction (see Fig. 13.47) or tenodesis using the ECU tendon. Ligament reconstruction is an anatomically sound solution for chronic, dynamic LT dissociation because it replaces the most important stabilizer of the joint—the palmar LT ligament.

![[Fig.13.47.png]]
> **Fig. 13.47** A 44-year-old policeman who was injured in a fight had painful chronic dynamic lunotriquetral dissociation. A complete nonrepairable LT injury was found arthroscopically (A), so it was decided to perform an open tendon reconstruction of the palmar (B) and dorsal (C) LT ligaments using a slip of the extensor carpi ulnaris tendon, according to Shin and colleagues.201 The patient had regained normal hand function 6 months after surgery with 35% reduced flexion but a strong and painless grip.

We seldom perform LT fusions, owing to the substantial rate of complications. When there is no other alternative than fusing the joint, we prefer the method described by Guidera et al., in which the empty space left in the LT joint, after resecting cartilage and subchondral bone from the central portion of the two articular surfaces, is filled with abundant, tightly packed cancellous bone graft obtained from the distal radius. The procedure is modified by the addition of a single cannulated screw to one or more Kirschner wires.

For patients with stage 3 static deformities, we recommend against an LT fusion. Our first choice would be RL fusion for static VISI deformity. Others prefer midcarpal fusion for static VISI deformity.

## NONDISSOCIATIVE CARPAL INSTABILITY (CIND)

A painful wrist is said to have a nondissociative pattern of instability when there is dysfunction of the radiocarpal and/or the midcarpal joints with complete integrity of the interosseous ligaments connecting the bones within the proximal row. Depending on which joints are affected, CIND problems can be further subdivided into radiocarpal, combined radiocarpal- midcarpal (or more simply, proximal row instability), and rarely pure midcarpal instability (Fig. 13.48). Proximal row instability has two distinct varieties: CIND- VISI (i.e., flexion rotation of the proximal row with a tendency of the capitate to subluxate volarly) and CIND- DISI (i.e., extension rotation of the proximal row with a dorsal capitate subluxation).

![[Fig.13.48.png]]
> **Fig. 13.48** Simplified representation of the four types of carpal instability nondissociative (CIND), and the most common displacements involved (arrows). A, Radiocarpal instability, in which the entire carpus is dissociated from the radius and ulna and may be displaced in different directions, most commonly ulnarly. B, Proximal row instability is manifested at both radiocarpal and midcarpal joints simultaneously. The proximal row rotates either volarly (CIND-VISI) or dorsally (CIND-DISI). C, Pure midcarpal instability (CIND-MC): This is an uncommon type in which only the midcarpal joint is unstable, the radiocarpal joint being normal, or following fusion to the radius.

### Radiocarpal Instability

Nondissociative radiocarpal instability occurs in patients with excessive laxity, insufficiency, or rupture of the radiocarpal ligaments. In these cases, the entire carpus can be displaced beyond normal limits down the volar or ulnar inclination of the radius. With time, further stretching of the capsule may occur, and the carpus becomes permanently translocated. This condition is most commonly seen in patients with inflammatory arthritis (Fig. 13.49) or in patients with developmental deformities such as the Madelung deformity. Posttraumatic radiocarpal instability is less frequent, most often associated with a radiocarpal dislocation. In patients with inflammatory arthritis, the radiocarpal ligaments fail as a result of the attritional effects of synovitis.

![[Fig.13.49.png]]
> **Fig. 13.49** Ulnar translocation in a 50-year-old woman with rheumatoid arthritis. A, Widened radial styloidscaphoid space (white double arrow). B, This unstable situation can be reduced in acute stages by laterally directed pressure (black arrow).

Traumatic ulnar translocation of the carpus caused by complete disruption of the extrinsic ligaments was described by Rayhack and associates. Taleisnik described two types of ulnar translocations. Type I involves displacement of the entire carpus, including the scaphoid, and the distance between the radial styloid process and the scaphoid is widened (see Fig. 13.49). In type II, the relationship between the distal row, the scaphoid, and the radius remains normal. The SL space is widened and the LT complex is ulnarly translocated (see Fig. 13.38).

![[Fig.13.38.png]]
> **Fig. 13.38** Tendon reconstructions of scapholunate (SL) dissociations can only be effective if the lunate is stable. An associated ulnar translation of the carpus will lead to failure. When evaluating an SL dissociation, therefore, it is imperative to rule out this problem, as emphasized by Taleisnik in 1988. The red arrow indicates subluxation of the scaphoid and the yellow arrow indicates ulnar translocation of the lunate.

The distinction between type I and type II is important for various reasons. Conceptually, type I is a true CIND instability, whereas type II has features of both CIND (ulnar translocation of the entire carpus *except* the scaphoid) and CID (SLD), and this makes it a *complex*

(CIC) pattern of instability (see Table 13.3). In practice, distinguishing between the two types is essential because different ligaments are involved that require different treatment; type I injuries result from failure of all radiocarpal ligaments, including the DRC, short and long RL and RSC. In type II injuries, the RS capsule and RS ligament remain intact; however, there is complete SL, DRC, and short/long RL ligament disruption. When presented with a widened SL joint, the possibility of a type II injury must be entertained, and appropriate high- resolution imaging or arthroscopy performed. If a type II ulnar translocation or reducible DISI deformity is treated by stabilizing only the apparent SLD, the underlying radiocarpal instability remains unresolved. If easily reducible, a procedure that has the potential to realign and stabilize the translocated intercalated segment may be indicated (see Stage 5 Scapholunate Dissociation, page 524, and CIC treatment, page 545).

As described in the discussion of the radiographic examination of carpal instabilities, a variety of methods for assessing ulnar translation have been reported (see Fig. 13.19B). None is universally reliable. The indices using the center of the capitate head as a carpal reference should not be used in type II injuries because in those cases only the LT complex is significantly displaced. On the other hand, when using the lunate as a reference, if the wrist is slightly radially or ulnarly deviated, the measurements may be unreliable. Dynamic stress views under fluoroscopy are useful in the assessment of this type of instability. When the joint is axially loaded in radial deviation, the proximal row is brought into maximal flexion. If the DRC is disrupted or elongated, the axial load induces abnormal ulnar translation of the lunate beyond the sigmoid notch of the radius.

![[Fig.13.19.png]]
> **Fig. 13.19** A, Carpal height ratio is calculated by dividing the carpal height (L2) by the length of the third metacarpal (L1). The normal ratio is 0.54 ± 0.03.117. B, To measure lunate translocation, Bouman and associates described a ratio of a line measuring the length of the articular surface of the radius (R) divided by the distance from the radial styloid to the proximal ulnar corner of the lunate (P-Lu). In normal wrists, this equates to 0.87 ± 0.04. This method is dependent on the wrist being precisely positioned in neutral as well as normal distal radius and carpal anatomy as any changes in these parameters can affect the measurements. (B, From Bouman HW, Messer E, Sennwald G. Measurement of ulnar translation and carpal height. J Hand Surg Br 19[3]:325–329, 1994.)

Rayhack and associates reported a single series of 8 patients with traumatic ulnar translocation of the carpus. Their experience indicates that this is a difficult problem to treat. They had disappointing results with delayed ligament repairs and suggested that probably a radiocarpal fusion is the only reliable alternative for such patients (see Fig. 13.41).

![[Fig.13.41.png]]
> **Fig. 13.41** Schematic representation (A) and case example (B) of a radioscapholunate fusion with distal scaphoidectomy: an option when a scapholunate dissociation is not easily reducible and the cartilage at the midcarpal joint is intact. The technique preserves the important midcarpal joint and the dart-thrower’s motion. Distal scaphoid excision allows increased radioulnar deviation, and the triquetrum can be excised at the surgeon’s discretion.

### Proximal Row Instability

Previously referred to as “palmar,” “anterior,” or “midcarpal instability,” this condition was first described in 1934 by Mouchet and Belot and is the most common of the CIND instability patterns. Proximal carpal row instability involves pathology of the ligaments crossing both the radiocarpal and midcarpal joints and can also be considered “combined radiocarpal/midcarpal instability.” It is usually seen in patients with congenital or acquired ligament laxity, is frequently bilateral, and most frequently presents with volar rotation of the entire proximal row (CIND- VISI). It may also occur by attenuation or rupture of the palmar midcarpal (THC, STT, and SC) ligaments as well as the DRC.In the VISI variant, as the hand is passively moved in the coronal plane from radial to ulnar deviation, the proximal row remains flexed until near the end, when it suddenly snaps into extension. This phenomenon has been termed the “catch- up” clunk (see Video 13.5). While some affected individuals are relatively asymptomatic, patients complain of a painful “clunk” when the wrist moves into ulnar deviation during activities such as throwing or pouring water from a jug. Rarely, proximal carpal row instability can present as a CIND- DISI variant, but this is nearly always posttraumatic. In this situation, the entire proximal carpal row rests in extension, secondary to injury of the dorsal intercarpal and long RL ligament insertions on the lunate. In rare atraumatic dorsal instability of the proximal row, the proximal row appears normally aligned except in ulnar deviation, where a dorsal subluxation of the capitate may appear, often with a clunk, as the lunate flips into DISI alignment. These have also been called “dorsal midcarpal instability” in the past. The dorsal subluxation of the capitate imparts an extension moment on the lunate. The posterior drawer test is useful to assess this type of condition and is often seen in patients with ligamentous laxity (see below).

#### Pathomechanics

As discussed previously, under axial compressive load, the proximal row tends to rotate into flexion and pronation, a tendency that is normally constrained by the palmar midcarpal ligaments and the DRC.Especially important palmar midcarpal stabilizers are the palmar TqH ligament, the palmar TC ligament, the radiopalmar STT ligament, and the SC ligament. These ligaments not only play a key role in preventing proximal row collapse but also are essential to ensure a smooth, progressive transition of the proximal row from flexion to extension as the wrist deviates ulnarly. Consequently, any injury or condition resulting in increased laxity of the TH, TC, STT, and SC ligaments is likely to have two consequences: (1) a kinetic instability (i.e., loss of the ability to transfer loads adequately without collapsing) and (2) a kinematic problem in which the transition of the proximal row from flexion to extension is sudden and sometimes painful, rather than smooth and progressive, when the wrist reaches a certain amount of ulnar deviation. In the laboratory, several cadaveric studies have addressed the intricate mechanism of ligament stabilization of the proximal row. The consequences of sectioning the radiocarpal and midcarpal ligaments are better understood but, comparatively, little is known about what causes these ligaments to fail.

#### Diagnosis

Proximal row instability seldom results from injury to one specific ligament. Most have congenital laxity with poor neuromuscular control, plus some sort of repetitive minor or single traumatic episode that initiates the symptoms. In these cases, there is an obvious sag of the wrist in the palmar direction, indicating a palmar translation plus supination of the distal row relative to the forearm—a malalignment that corrects itself in ulnar deviation.

#### Physical Examination

A useful maneuver to determine the amount of radiocarpal and midcarpal joint laxity is the *midcarpal shift test,* described by Lichtman and colleagues. It consists of reproducing the painful “catch- up clunk” by passively palmar translating the carpus, while moving the pronated wrist in the coronal plane from a position of radial deviation to ulnar deviation (Fig. 13.50; see Video 13.5). Based on how much force is necessary to maintain the wrist palmarly subluxated in ulnar deviation, wrists have been classified into five grades. In grade I, the palmar midcarpal ligaments are so tight that the distal row can hardly be displaced palmarly. Grades II and III can still be found in normal individuals and represent increasing levels of midcarpal laxity allowing the palmar sag to be obtained in ulnar inclination, although it reduces when the applied force is removed. In grade IV, subluxation is easily achieved, and the wrist remains subluxated after removal of the external force. Grade V instability occurs when the patient can actively reproduce and maintain the palmar sag in ulnar inclination without assistance from the examiner. Hargreaves developed an additional system that also helps guide treatment (Table 13.6).

![[Fig.13.50.png]]
> **Fig. 13.50** When the wrist inclines ulnarly, the proximal row undergoes a progressive extension. In some cases, however, that extension is not smooth and progressive—that is, the proximal row suddenly snaps into extension at the end of ulnar inclination. It is the so-called catch-up clunk.

**TABLE 13.6 Palmar Midcarpal Instability**

| Grade | Type | Description | Surgical Treatment Options |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Presymptomatic | No symptoms of instability but able to perform voluntary catch-up clunk. | No treatment |
| 1 | Dynamic | Symptoms of giving way. Symptoms reproduced with a positive midcarpal shift test. No voluntary clunk or sag. | Soft tissue stabilization or arthroscopic capsular shrinkage |
| 2 | Voluntary dynamic | Symptoms of giving way with voluntary subluxation (ulnar sag sign or dynamic catch-up clunk). | Soft tissue stabilization or arthroscopic capsular shrinkage |
| 3 | Static reducible | VISI deformity on lateral x-ray at rest. Deformity easily reducible on manipulation. | Soft tissue stabilization or bone procedure |
| 4 | Static irreducible | Fixed VISI deformity on lateral x-ray at rest. Not easily reducible. Locked. | Bone procedure |

- Posterior drawer test (CIND- DISI): If the capitate can be passively

#### Radiographic Examination

Radiographs usually show a VISI pattern, especially on unsupported lateral views compared with the opposite wrist. In the frontal view, the scaphoid is foreshortened and exhibits the ring sign, whereas the lunate has a moon- like appearance, its concave surface facing the scaphoid. In the atraumatic CIND- DISI wrists, proximal carpal row posture is usually normal.

Dynamic fluoroscopy can be helpful. In a normal wrist, there is synchronized motion of the proximal carpal row from flexion to extension as the wrist inclines ulnarly. In the wrist with CIND- VISI, the proximal carpal row remains flexed throughout the entire ROM except when the wrist reaches a certain ulnarly inclined position, at which point it suddenly snaps into an extended position, sometimes with an audible and palpable “clunk” (see Video 13.5).

Stress views are also useful to determine the degree of laxity or ligament insufficiency of the various radiocarpal and midcarpal joint stabilizers. A traction view is useful to rule out disruptions between the bones of the proximal row.

Four other stress views are recommended:

- Anterior drawer test (CIND- VISI): Under fluoroscopy control,

the distal row is translated palmarly and the relationship between the scaphoid, lunate, and the distal row is observed. If the STT joint widens palmarly (the so- called open mouth sign or yawning sign) (Fig. 13.51), both the STT and SC ligaments are probably stretched or disrupted.

![[Fig.13.51.png]]
> **Fig. 13.51** Stress views are particularly important to assess dynamic instabilities. In a 23-year-old patient, a combination of traction (black arrow) and ulnar inclination (UD) showed an increased scaphotrapezial gap (white arrow), “yawn sign,” which was not present on the contralateral side. Because this finding coincided with the location of major tenderness, a ligament injury was suspected and treated nonoperatively.

translocated beyond the dorsal edge of the lunate with manual dorsal translation of the hand while stabilizing the radius, the DIC is probably lax or attenuated. In ulnar deviation, slight dorsal subluxation of the capitate may appear. Proximal row alignment should return back to normal once the dorsal directed force is released. When minimal force is necessary to dislocate the capitate in neutral position, a posterior midcarpal instability (CLIP wrist) is more likely (see page 542).

- Forced ulnar deviation test: When the wrist is forced into extreme

ulnar deviation, if the STT and SC ligaments are torn or insufficient, the scaphoid remains foreshortened while a gap appears at the joint (see Fig. 13.51). Any asymmetric gap beyond 4 mm is indicative of STT and SC laxity or injury.

![[Fig.13.51.png]]
> **Fig. 13.51** Stress views are particularly important to assess dynamic instabilities. In a 23-year-old patient, a combination of traction (black arrow) and ulnar inclination (UD) showed an increased scaphotrapezial gap (white arrow), “yawn sign,” which was not present on the contralateral side. Because this finding coincided with the location of major tenderness, a ligament injury was suspected and treated nonoperatively.

- Forced radial deviation test: When the joint is axially loaded in

radial deviation, the proximal row is brought into maximal flexion. In cases of radiocarpal instability the axial load induces abnormal ulnar translation of the lunate beyond the sigmoid notch of the radius.

Advanced imaging such as US or MRI may help better understand the relative integrity of the volar arcuate ligament and to rule out dissociative instability.

Diagnostic arthroscopy can be utilized to visualize the involved ligaments. In lax wrists, the elongated palmar capsule allows greater joint separation than normal, but the palmar ligaments usually look normal. Oftentimes, diffuse proliferative synovitis is noted especially within the TH joint, which may also appear wide. Using a probe, one can appreciate their patulous, attenuated nature. In long- standing cases, recurrent subluxation may have caused degenerative changes at the proximal pole of the hamate, which can be assessed and treated arthroscopically with debridement or drilling. Arthroscopy also enables the surgeon to rule out other associated diagnoses such as dissociative instability and disorders of the TFCC that may present with ulnar-sided wrist pain (see Chapter 17).

#### Differential Diagnosis

When the radiographs of a clunking wrist reveal abnormal flexion of the proximal row, the differential diagnosis must include LT instability. The diagnosis can be established by using the LT ballottement and shear maneuvers. Both tests are negative in nondissociative instabilities and induce pain and a typical grinding sensation when there is LT dissociation. Radiographically, the SL and LT angles are normal in nondissociative instabilities, whereas they may be altered in wrists with dissociative instability (see Fig. 13.46). When the LT ligaments are completely torn, the triquetrum appears proximally migrated relative to the lunate in the ulnar deviation stress test; this does not occur in a wrist with nondissociative instability. Highresolution MRI can also be very useful. When in doubt, arthroscopy is recommended. Inspection with a probe shows a tightly closed LT intercarpal space in nondissociative instabilities and a space easily opened up if there is substantial ligament damage of the LTstabilizing ligaments.

![[Fig.13.46.png]]
> **Fig. 13.46** A, The lunotriquetral angle is difficult to identify in a lateral radiograph of a normal wrist, and its quantification difficult to reproduce. Yet identifying the relative angle of the triquetral (Tq) axis relative to the lunate axis may be of help to discriminate between a (B) carpal instability nondissociative (CIND-VISI) wrist and a wrist with (C) LT dissociation. Both in normal wrists and in CIND-VISI wrists, the triquetral axis is angled volar to the lunate’s axis. In the LT dissociated wrist, by contrast, the triquetral axis is always angled dorsal to the lunate’s longitudinal axis.

#### Treatment

The natural history of CIND- VISI is unknown. In patients with painful CIND- VISI, initial treatment should consist of splint immobilization for 6 to 8 weeks, antiinflammatory medication, and avoidance of activities that reproduce the painful clunk. In the initial inflammatory phase, the wrist should be treated with a so- called pisiform boost splint, a palmar wrist splint with padding to the volar surface of the pisiform that maintains the proximal row neutrally aligned and prevents the catch- up clunk (Fig. 13.52). When symptoms have subsided, a supervised therapy program to reestablish adequate proprioceptive control of the wrist may help reduce symptoms. Excessive flexion of the proximal row may be improved by rehabilitation of the coupled action of the FCU and ECU muscles. Isometric contraction of the FCU muscle generates a dorsally directed force onto the triquetrum via the pisiform, which helps stabilize the proximal row in a neutral position. Concomitant isometric contraction of the ECU pronates the distal row relative to the proximal row, further promoting reduction of the VISI malalignment. Poor proprioception and inadequate neuromuscular control may differentiate symptomatic from asymptomatic wrists.

![[Fig.13.52.png]]
> **Fig. 13.52** In the initial phase, wrists with a painful CIND-VISI instability should be treated with a so-called pisiform boost splint—a three-point (red arrows) gutter splint (A) with padding on the volar surface of the pisiform that maintains the proximal row neutrally aligned and prevents the “catch-up clunk” (B).

Patients with CIND- DISI should always undergo a dedicated program of physical rehabilitation. In this case hand therapy should emphasize proprioceptive reeducation of the ECU, ECRL, and ECRB muscles. When these muscles contract, an extension moment to the distal row is generated that results in a shift of the midcarpal joint contact forces from a dorsal position to a more palmar one. Such compressive forces prevent excessive extension of the proximal row.

When all conservative measures have failed to control the symptoms of patients with CIND- VISI, the following treatment options exist.

**STT ligament reconstruction.** When the primary pathology is determined to be at the STT joint, two basic strategies have been proposed: a soft- tissue reconstruction of the STT ligaments or a limited intercarpal fusion. Certainly, these are short structures, difficult to reconstruct in the chronic situation as it is not rare to find retraction and degeneration of these ligaments. In such circumstances, the tenodesis technique described by Brunelli and Brunelli may be considered. The procedure consists of obtaining a slip of FCR, leaving it attached distally, and passing it through a tunnel across the distal scaphoid, thus roughly replicating the direction of the STT ligaments. For this to be successful, it is mandatory that the cartilage at the joint be normal, otherwise painful ankylosis may follow. If the STT joint is already degenerated, but the proximal pole of the scaphoid still has good cartilage, an STT fusion is a good alternative.

**Capsular shrinkage.** Based on experience with other joints, electrothermal shrinkage of insufficient or stretched ligaments has been proposed for the stabilization of CIND- VISI. Local heat is applied to the loose palmar midcarpal and DRC capsule to create areas of collagen damage. Fibroblasts grow into the injured tissues, building a new structure simulating normal tissue. Hargreaves conducted a retrospective review of 13 patients at an average of 4 years of follow- up and noted an improvement in the frequency of the instability symptoms, an improvement of DASH scores, and a 15% loss of wrist motion. Despite some promising reports, the use of capsular shrinkage can generate inconsistent results. The reader is referred to Chapter 17 for further details about this arthroscopic technique and an open treatment algorithm for CIND- VISI.

**Soft tissue ligament reconstruction.** In the past, most attempts to resolve CIND- VISI through a soft tissue procedure were based on the assumption that the underlying pathology was an elongation of the DRC, TqH, and TC ligaments. In most instances, surgery consisted of reefing or advancing the portion of capsule that contains these ligaments or reconstructing them with a tendon graft; however, longterm results are lacking. We believe that it is implausible that a tendon graft can adequately simulate the mechanical properties of these highly specialized and tensioned ligaments over a prolonged period and heavy use. von Schroeder reported on the outcomes of 23 patients with 27 symptomatic CIND wrists who underwent dorsal plication of the capsule containing portions of the DRC and DIC ligaments.All treated patients had failed 6 weeks of nonoperative treatment. At a follow- up of 35 months, all patients had improved pain although the more predictable results were noted in hypermobile, female patients under 25 years. Male patients aged over 25 years who had moderate or severe trauma had worse outcomes and 2 went on to a four- corner fusion. Patients with CIND- DISI who fail proprioceptive reeducation may be candidates for a transverse dorsal midcarpal capsulodesis to reinforce the dorsal intercarpal ligament, as suggested by Gajendran and colleagues.

Loisel and colleagues reported the outcomes of 8 patients from three centers who developed *traumatic* nondissociative DISI (n = 2) or nondissociative VISI (n = 6) following treatment of a nondisplaced waist fracture of the scaphoid. In three cases diagnosed within 12 weeks of injury, treatment with ligament repair was successful in restoring RL angle to normal. Late treatment in five patients was unsuccessful and required secondary salvage procedures. The authors proposed that posttraumatic CIND- DISI or VISI required the disruption of at least one radiocarpal and one midcarpal critical ligament stabilizer from both the dorsal and palmar sides (see Fig. 13.11), and that early recognition using high- resolution MRI and/or arthroscopy is necessary to effectively diagnose and treat this rare instability pattern.

![[Fig.13.11.png]]
> **Fig. 13.11** A, Like the most stable configuration of a boat at mooring, the lunate has four “critical ligament stabilizers” (two dorsal, two palmar, two radiocarpal, and two midcarpal) ensuring its postural stability, even when the scapholunate or lunotriquetral ligaments have been divided in isolation. B, Ruptures or attenuation of one or more of these ligament stabilizers lead to dissociative or nondissociative postural deformities of the bones of the proximal carpal row. DIC, Dorsal intercarpal ligament; DISI, dorsal intercalated segmental instability; DRC, dorsal radiocarpal; LRL, long radiolunate; STT, scaphotrapeziotrapezoid; SC, scaphocapitate; VISI, volar intercalated segmental instability. (B, Copyright © Gregory I. Bain, FRACS, PhD.)

**Intercarpal arthrodesis.** Midcarpal arthrodeses (triquetrumhamate or four- corner fusions) have also been recommended for CIND-VISI (Fig. 13.53). In a study comparing the results of fusions with those of a variety of soft tissue reconstructions, Lichtman and colleagues confirmed the advantage of the former in the treatment of patients with CIND- VISI. All of them were able to return to their normal level of activity with less than one- third loss of motion and no clunking. However, later publications have been less enthusiastic about such an approach. Although this type of procedure is effective in eliminating the carpal clunking, this may be achieved at the expense of creating a painful RS impingement. Furthermore, when the midcarpal joint is fused, DT motion is inhibited and the radiocarpal joint cannot adequately accommodate; this eventually results in either a dorsolateral overload of the scaphoid fossa or a progressive deterioration of the LT. More recently, it has been suggested that carpal clunking may also be eliminated by fusing the RL joint instead of the midcarpal joint. By fixing the lunate to the radius, the cornerstone of the carpus is placed in a neutral position, allowing more stable midcarpal motion. The lunate is fused with crossed Kirschner wires and a corticocancellous graft to preserve normal carpal height, allowing the scaphoid and triquetrum to move normally. Not only does the clunking disappear but also the wrist retains a substantial amount of motion, mostly along the dart- thrower’s plane. Certainly, RL fusion allows a more natural way of moving the wrist. Shiga et al. conducted a biomechanical analysis of palmar midcarpal instability treated with either an RL arthrodesis or TqH fusion and noted while both procedures eliminated the clunking, the former reduced scaphoid flexion whereas the latter altered proximal row motion in several directions. The longterm clinical effects of this need further evaluation.

![[Fig.13.53.png]]
> **Fig. 13.53** A 21-year-old man sustained a hyperextension injury to the right wrist 2 years earlier. Since then, there has been painful clunking during radioulnar inclinations. Dynamic radiographs showed a sudden shift (arrow) of the lunate and scaphoid from a flexed (A) to an extended (B) position as the wrist reached a certain degree of ulnar inclination. Conservative treatment did not succeed in relieving the patient’s symptoms, and a triquetrum-hamate fusion was performed. Four months after surgery, the patient was back to his former occupation without any complaint. At 18 months of follow-up, the clunking had not recurred but an obviously altered carpal kinematics was evident. From radial (C) to ulnar (D) inclination, little midcarpal motion exists. Whether such increased radiocarpal shear will result in early degeneration is unknown, but it is a good possibility.

### Midcarpal Instability (CLIP Wrist)

The term *midcarpal instability* should only be used to describe cases where the midcarpal joint is unstable but not the radiocarpal joint. In reality, most unstable midcarpal joints are associated with a variable degree of radiocarpal instability and the consequent VISI or DISI postural deformities; hence the more applicable term *proximal row instability.* One condition with potential for developing a pure midcarpal instability would be a previously operated wrist with incompetent midcarpal ligaments after radiocarpal fusion or a proximal wrist hemiarthroplasty. When loaded in full flexion or extension, those wrists could theoretically exhibit capitate subluxations or even dislocations over the edge of the fixed proximal row/radial implant.

The closest reported instability patterns that resemble pure midcarpal instability are the CLIP described by Louis et al. and the chronic capitolunate instability (CCI) pattern reported independently by Johnson and Carrera. In the series by Louis et al., 10 of 11 patients with dynamic “central carpal” instability manifested with painful snapping of the CL joint under load (but no lunate malposition) and were treated successfully with hand therapy and activity modification; one underwent dorsal midcarpal ligament imbrication. Diagnosis was based on subluxation of the capitate from the lunate fossa with a traction/dorsal displacement maneuver under fluoroscopy—the posterior drawer test. Lunate static and dynamic position was normal in all patients, confirming its isolation to the midcarpal joint.

In Johnson and Carrera’s series, 12 patients with chronic wrist pain and painful clicking were evaluated with a similar “dorsal capitatedisplacement apprehension test” under fluoroscopy. All patients had a prior history of wrist extension trauma and none had been able to return to forceful activities because of painful weakness. As was identified in the Louis test, all patients demonstrated capitate dorsal subluxation from the lunate, which reproduced their symptoms. None had lunate malalignment or evidence of carpal instability on plain or stress radiographs. Given their failure to improve with nonoperative treatment and therapy, 11 of 12 underwent an extended anterior approach to the carpal tunnel and suture imbrication of the sulcus that exists between the long RL and RSC ligaments (i.e., space of Poirier) to restrict passive extension to 30 degrees. At a minimum 2- year follow- up, seven patients considered their wrists “normal,” three had a good result, and one patient who underwent tendon graft imbrication had a poor result. Average wrist extension was 57 degrees, and the apprehension test was eliminated in all.

### AUTHORS' PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: NONDISSOCIATIVE INSTABILITY

Our assessment of painful clunking wrists is mostly based on a thorough physical examination, plus dynamic fluoroscopy and stress views. The goal is to rule out CID, determine the location and characteristics of the predominant dysfunction, and classify the type of instability. In case of doubt, arthroscopy may be used. In our experience, most atraumatic nondissociative disorders respond well to nonoperative treatment. This consists of splinting, avoidance of activities that produce the painful snapping, and a controlled hand therapy program aimed at maximizing the stabilizing action of the ECU and FCU. Surgery is only indicated when the conservative protocol fails to control the patient’s symptoms.

Through selective arthroscopic capsular shrinkage (palmar midcarpal and ulnocarpal, DRC and TH ligaments) and immobilization, we find that this treatment may be a useful adjunct in those patients with refractory symptoms, especially in the short and midterm. In terms of soft tissue reconstructions, an open discussion is had with the patients regarding the longevity of these procedures given the concern of soft tissue creep over time, especially in patients with ligament laxity or collagen-vascular disorders (e.g., Ehlers-Danlos syndrome). In our experience, most patients are initially reluctant to undergo limited arthrodesis and see that as a last resort. As such, we favor procedures that plicate or reconstruct the DRC ligament with additional suture reinforcement.

For the treatment of atraumatic proximal row instability (CINDVISI), Garcia- Elias and Lluch published a tendon graft tenodesis procedure in the previous edition of this book that is designed to recreate the ulnar arm of the arcuate ligament and augment the DRC (Fig. 13.54). Two longitudinal incisions (i.e., dorsal and palmar) are used. Two drill holes, 3.2 mm in diameter are made: (1) one from the dorsal aspect of the capitate into the carpal canal and (2) another from the palmar aspect of the triquetrum into its dorsal ridge. A strip of ECRB tendon is passed through the capitate hole, retrieved palmarly, and passed again through the triquetrum hole. The tendon is pulled taut, and sutures reinforcing the remnants of the palmar TC and TqH ligaments are placed. On the dorsum, the tendon is tightly sutured to the origin of the dorsal- radiocarpal ligament. Kirschner wires are used to stabilize the construct. While we have no experience with this technique, GarciaElias and Lluch noted that the results obtained in seven patients had been promising, with minimal discomfort, excellent grip strength, and minimal loss of motion. All patients returned to their occupations without clunking. This option is used only when the dysfunction is easily reducible, with perfectly normal cartilages. In all other instances, we recommend either a four- corner or an RL arthrodesis.

![[Fig.13.54.png]]
> **Fig. 13.54** A tenodesis procedure that reinforces the two major ligaments involved in CIND-VISI. A distally attached strip of the extensor carpi radialis brevis (ECRB) tendon is raised and passed through holes in the capitate and triquetrum (red arrows) to recreate the ulnar limb of the arcuate ligament (triquetrum-capitate bundles) and the dorsal radiocarpal ligament.

The authors tend to favor limited carpal fusions for CIND- VISI as the results tend to be more predictable in obliterating the clunking. Garcia- Elias reported successful treatment of 9 patients with an RL fusion, which eliminated the clunking. Most patients retained functional ROM through the DT plane. Our experience with the use of RL fusion for the treatment of proximal row instability, although limited in number, has been satisfying. Not only were nearly all patients able to return to their previous activities but also the range of flexion- extension at follow- up was only slightly reduced, grip strength was normal, and no clunking recurred. Indeed, patients are happy after an RL fusion because pain and instability disappear at the expense of losing only a moderate portion of wrist mobility.

In patients with traumatically induced asymmetric CIND-VISI or CIND-DISI following scaphoid or distal radius fractures, a high level of suspicion is necessary to detect and treat these deformities before fixed contracture ensues. Prompt high resolution MRI or arthroscopic inspection is helpful to identify injured critical ligaments, as early arthroscopic or open repair (within 12 weeks of injury) effectively corrected the postural deformity in one series.

When surgery is required for dorsal midcarpal (CLIP) instability, either obliteration of the space of Poirier with nonabsorbable sutures using Johnson and Carrera’s technique or a dorsal reinforcement of the midcarpal capsule by using a capsulodesis technique are effective and without considerable morbidity.

## CARPAL INSTABILITY ADAPTIVE (CIA)

The introduction of this chapter emphasizes that ligament injury, wrist instability, and carpal malalignment, although frequently associated, are independent events—the presence of one does not necessarily imply the presence of the other two. Indeed, not all wrists with a ligament injury develop carpal instability, and not all unstable wrists are malaligned. The previous section discussed carpal instabilities secondary to ligament injuries. This section reviews the adaptive malalignments (i.e., the carpal malalignment that is not caused by a ligament injury).

The concept of adaptive malalignment was introduced in 1984 by Allieu, emphasizing the fact that this type of dysfunction should not be confused with that secondary to ligament injury within the carpus. Taleisnik and Watson reported on 13 patients with dorsal malunions of the distal radius and secondary carpal dysfunction and malalignment which were solved after a corrective radial osteotomy. They used the term “extrinsic” instability to differentiate these cases from those carpal instabilities deriving from ligament pathology. For a diagnosis of adaptive carpus to be made, it is important to rule out the existence of an intrinsic or extrinsic ligament injury.

The most frequent radial malunion deformity involves radial shortening plus extension, radial deviation, and supination of the distal fragment relative to the radial shaft. Padmore and colleagues noted the mechanical effects on carpal kinematics with dorsally angulated distal radius deformities. Using a cadaver model, they noted that with increasing dorsal angulation, there was an increase in the percentage of motion within the radiocarpal joint and a concomitant decrease in wrist motion within the midcarpal joint. Clinically, if the distal radial surface is tilted dorsally, a neutral wrist posture will require increased flexion of the midcarpal joint. The result will be an overall loss of wrist flexion, which may prompt a consultation for treatment. Occasionally, patients may report a clunking sensation during ulnar deviation, which may represent some degree of capitate subluxation over the extended proximal carpal row. It is important to note that the primary cause of this type of kinematic dysfunction is not ligament insufficiency but radial deformity; therefore ligament repair is not indicated. The malalignment is an adaptive phenomenon that tends to improve when the distal radial articular surface is correctly aligned. Prolonged malposition of the carpus tends to stretch the dorsal capsuloligamentous structures with time, and the kinematic abnormalities may eventually become permanent. Detailed information about the planning and execution of a corrective osteotomy is provided in Chapter 15. Radial translocation instability is decidedly rare and usually associated with radial malunion and concomitant loss of radial height. In light of this, it is best considered an adaptive carpal instability. In this situation, the normal 23 degrees of ulnar inclination in the coronal plane is partially or completely reversed.

### Adaptive Carpal Malalignment Secondary to Scaphoid Nonunion

When an unstable wrist is axially loaded, the scaphoid prevents collapse of the central column. Scaphoid alignment is important but so is its geometry; a deformed scaphoid can create carpal collapse. If an untreated scaphoid nonunion collapses into a “humpback” deformity, with characteristic dorsal angulation and shortening, the necessary coordinated motion of the two carpal rows is lost, resulting in dorsal tilt of the proximal carpal row and abnormal kinematics of the carpus. Since there has been no disruption of an interosseous ligament of the proximal row, the shortening of the radial column and its resultant kinematic abnormalities can be technically considered an “adaptive” carpal instability. Prompt treatment of the scaphoid nonunion to restore its length and alignment is necessary to avoid scaphoid nonunion advanced collapse and posttraumatic arthritis. A thorough description of the techniques to achieve these goals is provided in Chapter 16.

### Adaptive Carpal Malalignment Secondary to STT Arthritis

Another rather frequent condition that causes scaphoid deformity, and secondarily nondissociative carpal malalignment, is STT osteoarthritis. STT arthritis begins as an isolated degenerative phenomenon, often bilateral, and may erode the cartilage and subchondral bone of the distal scaphoid. In such circumstances, the shorter the scaphoid, the more the proximal row extends to compensate for the defect, especially when there is a type 1 lunate. This is an adaptive carpal instability and may progress to frank DISI posture of the entire proximal row.In later stages, the condition can progress to advanced degenerative disease of the midcarpal joint, or so- called STTAC arthritis. Surgical treatment of STT induced DISI may require CL or four- corner arthrodesis.

### Carpal Malalignment Secondary to Deformity of the Lunate

Although Kienböck disease is discussed fully in Chapter 16 (see also online “Updates” chapter), a brief comment here about the peculiar features of the carpal instability that appear when the diseased lunate collapses is pertinent. One of the prognostic factors of Kienböck disease is the absence (stage 3A) or presence (stage 3B) of abnormal flexion and pronation deformity of the scaphoid. In stage 3A, the carpus remains relatively stable, whereas in stage 3B, it has collapsed. Fragmentation of the lunate results not only in loss of the mechanical strength of the central column but also in proximal migration of the capitate, which slackens the RSC and SC ligaments and leads to kinematic disruption of the carpus. In such circumstances, the loaded scaphoid is apt to follow its natural tendency and progressively collapse into flexion, another example of adaptive carpal instability. Rarely does Kienböck disease demonstrate SL gap, DISI, or dorsal translation of the scaphoid, the pathognomonic findings of dissociative instability.

## CARPAL INSTABILITY COMPLEX

### Definition

According to the Mayo Clinic classification, when there is complete rupture of the ligaments binding bones of the same row, the resultant instability fulfills the criteria of a CID. If the carpal disruption has preserved the ligaments that connect the bones of the same row but not the ones that stabilize the radiocarpal and/or midcarpal ligaments, it fulfills the criteria of a CIND. When a carpal injury shares features of both CID and CIND, the instability can be categorized as a “complex instability of the carpus” (CIC). Acute carpal fracture- dislocations are examples of CIC.

It is important to note that a dislocation is only unstable in the early stages of the injury, particularly if it has not been reduced. With time, the unreduced dislocation becomes stiff, and stiffness is the opposite of instability. Indeed, chronic unreduced fracture- dislocations should not be classified as a carpal instability problem but as a more complex injury that tends to progress to stiffness and degenerative disease.

There are six types of wrist dislocations:

1. Dorsal perilunate dislocations (lesser arc)
2. Dorsal perilunate fracture- dislocations (greater arc)
3. Palmar perilunate dislocations (lesser or greater arc)
4. Radiocarpal dislocations
5. Axial dislocations
6. Isolated carpal bone dislocations

The first two groups have in common a carpal dislocation occurring around the lunate; the first as a pure ligament disruption, the second involving one or more fractures of the adjacent bones. The third group, although perilunate, results from a different mechanism producing a palmar displacement of the distal row relative to the lunate. The fourth through sixth groups are nonperilunate dislocations, usually the result of high- energy trauma.

### Dorsal Perilunate Dislocations (Lesser- Arc Injuries)

Under the diagnosis of dorsal perilunate dislocation, there are various forms of carpal injury, all confined to a relatively vulnerable area around the lunate (Fig. 13.55). They can be categorized as purely ligamentous injuries or in combination with fractures of the bones around the lunate (see Chapter 16). Johnson suggested using the term *lesserarc injuries* to refer to pure perilunate ligament dislocations, as opposed to *greater- arc injuries,* in which one or several bones around the lunate have a concomitant fracture.

![[Fig.13.55.png]]
> **Fig. 13.55** Carpal dislocations constitute a spectrum of injury, and the initial lateral radiograph in a patient with carpal dislocation may depict a configuration at any point on that spectrum. A, “Pure” dorsal perilunate dislocation. B, Intermediate stage. C, “Pure” volar lunate dislocation.

Bain et al. have expanded this concept further by including the translunate arc, a particular type of wrist dislocation where the lunate has suffered a coronal fracture—the dorsal portion of it displaces together the distal row. Herzberg coined the term perilunate injury not dislocated (PLIND) as a variant of these injuries in which there is no dislocation of the capitate from the lunate on the initial radiographs. These injuries may reflect a lesser arc, greater arc, or translunate type. As such, a high index of suspicion needs to be maintained when evaluating these patients. This section focuses on lesser- arc injuries: dorsally displaced perilunate dislocations without an associated fracture.

Throughout the literature, there has been a tendency to consider dorsal perilunate dislocations and palmar- lunate dislocations as separate and distinct entities. In reality, the two conditions represent different stages of the same injury—the so- called progressive perilunate instability pattern. When one attempts to reduce a palmarly dislocated lunate, a dorsal perilunate dislocation can be easily reproduced. In other words, the position of the bones when the patient is seen in the emergency department does not reflect the degree of instability or the full extent of the ligamentous damage. Perilunate and lunate dislocations are pathogenically equivalent lesions, and their management is almost identical.

Another frequent misconception is that when the LC joint dislocates (i.e., the Mayfield stage II), the scaphoid has either fractured or torn its ligamentous attachments to the lunate, but not both. In the literature, perilunate dislocations with a simultaneous scaphoid fracture and SLD have been reported, showing that the two lesions are not mutually exclusive. Most experimentally produced scaphoid fractures have an associated partial SL intercarpal ligament failure. In a multicenter study of 166 perilunate dislocations, Herzberg and coworkers found 6 cases (3.8%) with a concomitant scaphoid fracture and SLD (Fig. 13.56).

![[Fig.13.56.png]]
> **Fig. 13.56** Posteroanterior (A) and lateral (B) radiographs, as well as 3D reconstruction of a computed tomography scan seen from both the volar (C) and dorsal (D) sides of the wrist of a patient who sustained a transscaphoid perilunate dislocation with dorsal enucleation of the proximal pole of the scaphoid.

#### Treatment of Perilunate Dislocations

In the literature, three major methods of treating carpal dislocations have been suggested: closed reduction and cast immobilization, closed reduction and percutaneous pinning under arthroscopy control, and open reduction and internal fixation.

**Closed reduction and cast immobilization.** Since the 1920s, when case reports of lunate dislocations began to appear, many methods have been suggested for closed reduction. Some authors have recommended continuous traction before attempting to reduce the dislocation, while others have used a spanning external fixator to neutralize muscle contraction and apply ligamentotaxis.

Complete muscle relaxation is essential for an atraumatic reduction of a carpal dislocation. General anesthesia, axillary block, conscious sedation, and intravenous regional anesthesia (i.e., Bier block) all provide satisfactory muscle relaxation. Local anesthesia or a “hematoma block” does not. An initial period of 10 minutes of uninterrupted traction with the elbow flexed 90 degrees is helpful before reduction begins. During traction, PA and lateral radiographs with the carpus distracted are obtained. These films are valuable in delineating the full extent of carpal damage. When the wrist has been distracted for 10 minutes, traction is released, and the method of reduction described by Tavernier is attempted as follows (Fig. 13.57).

![[Fig.13.57.png]]
> **Fig. 13.57** Schematic representation of Tavernier’s method of reduction of dorsal perilunate dislocations. With the wrist slightly extended, gentle manual traction is applied (1). Without releasing such traction, and while the lunate is stabilized palmarly by the surgeon’s thumb, the wrist is flexed until a reduction clunk occurs (2). This indicates that the proximal pole of the capitate has engaged the dorsal horn of the lunate. At this point, traction is released, and the wrist is brought back to neutral (3).

With one hand, the patient’s wrist is extended (maintaining longitudinal traction), while the thumb of the other hand stabilizes the lunate on the palmar aspect of the wrist. Gradual flexion of the wrist allows the capitate to reduce back into the lunate concavity. To facilitate this maneuver, the operator’s thumb stabilizes the lunate to prevent it from being displaced forward by the capitate. When the LC joint is reduced, and without releasing traction, the wrist is extended gradually while the lunate is pushed dorsally with the thumb, and a full reduction is usually achieved. The sooner after injury this technique is performed, the easier it is to do the reduction.

Postreduction radiographs and a CT scan are taken. It is imperative to take multiple different views and to assess “very critically” the relationship of the capitate and lunate and the position of the scaphoid. An SL angle greater than 80 degrees and an SL gap greater than 3 mm have been shown to indicate a poor prognosis if not surgically corrected. The wrist may be initially immobilized with a dorsal short- arm and thumb spica plaster splint with the wrist in neutral. Unless operative treatment is medically contraindicated, surgery should be done expeditiously. In the rare situation in which closed reduction alone is necessitated, at least 12 weeks of immobilization is recommended, with weekly radiographic reassessment of the reduction for at least the first 3 weeks. Closed treatment should be continued only if medical reasons contraindicate surgical intervention.

**Closed reduction and percutaneous ﬁxation under arthroscopic control.** Percutaneous pin fixation is acceptable only if the reduction achieved by closed manipulation of all the perilunate joints is completely anatomic. Minor degrees of scaphoid malrotation would prevent the SL ligaments from being in contact for proper healing, leading to later SL or LT instability, or both (Fig. 13.58). The introduction of arthroscopy to ensure proper reduction of the carpal displacements has made this alternative more attractive. The technique of percutaneous fixation is as follows. (Please refer to Chapter 17 for further details on the role of arthroscopic management of perilunate injuries.)

![[Fig.13.58.png]]
> **Fig. 13.58** Nonoperative treatment of this dorsal perilunate dislocation, which consisted of reduction, cast immobilization for 7 weeks, and physical rehabilitation, was considered successful until the patient was assessed 8 years after the injury. A to C, Chronic scapholunate dissociation (black arrow) associated with ulnar translation of the lunate (white arrow) was the result of this suboptimal reduction. The wrist was not unstable; however, it continued to be stiff and painful.

After an initial reduction, the hand is prepared in a standard aseptic manner. The procedure starts by inserting three Kirschner wires dorsally, one into the lunate, one into the scaphoid, and the other into the triquetrum, under oscillate mode; they are used as joysticks to manipulate and align the carpus into an anatomic reduction. Dry arthroscopy with automated washout is performed in the radiocarpal and midcarpal joints to diagnose the associated carpal and ligament injuries, debride joint debris, and verify carpal bone alignment and carpal posture with the image intensifier. While a knowledgeable assistant holds the lunate wire in the reduced position relative to the radius, a 0.045- inch or 0.062- inch oblique pin from the radial aspect of the radial metaphysis is inserted across the RL joint under fluoroscopy.

The scaphoid joystick wire is used to reduce the SL joint anatomically, followed by arthroscopic confirmation of anatomic alignment (camera in the ulna midcarpal portal), and two slightly divergent pins or a temporary cannulated screw are inserted transversely across the SL joint through the anatomic snuffbox. The wires should be placed through a small open incision using drill sleeves to minimize risk to the radial artery and radial sensory nerve branches. An arthroscopic probe placed through the radial midcarpal portal can further assist with closed reduction of the SL joint. The arthroscope is now placed in the radial midcarpal portal and the probe in the ulnar midcarpal portal, respectively, to reduce the triquetrum to the lunate. Two more Kirschner wires or a temporary cannulated screw are inserted through a small incision from the anteromedial corner of the triquetrum across the LT joint.

LC joint mobility is inspected under fluoroscopy. If there is a tendency for the capitate to subluxate dorsally in flexion, a further pin is passed across the SC joint. The Kirschner wires are left protruding through the skin, bent at right angles, or cut just under the skin to facilitate later removal. One of the advantages of dry arthroscopy is the ease to facilitate fixation of concomitant fractures as needed with minimal disruption of tissue planes through an open approach (e.g., scaphoid fractures). A padded thumb spica splint is applied immediately after the final radiographs have been obtained. This is converted to a thumb spica cast at 10 to 14 days after swelling has subsided and assuming the pin tracts are free of infection. Radiographs are taken in the new cast to ensure proper maintenance of reduction. The cast and pins are removed at 10 to 11 weeks, and the patient is transitioned to a removable splint with dedicated hand therapy. This technique is predicated on the surgeon having knowledge of the fluoroscopic and arthroscopic reduction of the carpus. Herzberg et al. reported similar outcomes of arthroscopic and open techniques. Kim and colleagues treated 20 patients arthroscopically with acute lesser or greater arc injuries and found that at an average of 31 months, there were 3 excellent, 8 good, 7 fair, and 2 poor outcomes according to the Mayo Wrist score. One drawback of this technique is the fact that it does not allow repair of the critical dorsal component of the SL ligament, a factor that has been associated with worse long- term outcomes.

Suboptimal reductions of perilunate dislocations will inevitably lead to poor long- term results. Even when an acceptable restoration of the anatomy has been obtained, one must consider the benefits of an open procedure to ensure adequate repair of the dorsal SL interosseous ligament and to avoid missing osteochondral defects and loose intraarticular bone or cartilage fragments that may lead to poor outcomes.

**Open reduction and internal ﬁxation.** In general it is believed that open surgery of a carpal dislocation is likely to achieve better results than closed treatment because it allows (1) complete recognition of all bone and soft tissue damage, (2) removal of interposed soft tissue, (3) removal of unstable chondral fragments, (4) an accurate reduction of bone displacement, and (5) suture of repairable ligaments (Fig. 13.59). Many long- term follow- up studies have shown the superiority of open reduction, ligament repair, and percutaneous Kirschner wire fixation over any other alternative in the treatment of perilunate dislocations. Although some authors still prefer using only a dorsal approach to treat perilunate dislocations, the alternative of combining a dorsal and a palmar approach to allow repair of the palmar LT ligament while assessing the reduction and repair of the dSLIL from the dorsum is gaining wider recognition. Certainly an unreduced lunate dislocation or severe median nerve deficit will require an open palmar incision. If choosing a dual incision, one needs to consider the morbidity of the soft tissue stripping to an already compromised carpus, which can result in avascular necrosis or the lunate.

![[Fig.13.59.png]]
> **Fig. 13.59** A 31-year-old male professional motorcycle racer fell during a race. A, Computed tomography scan and 3D reconstruction of unreduced perilunate dislocation obtained 3 days after the injury. B, Surgical approach using an extended carpal tunnel incision allows visualization of the distal surface of the lunate. After reduction, the disrupted palmar lunotriquetral ligament (arrow) was repaired adding further stability to the proximal row. C, Placement of stabilizing Kirschner wires across the scapholunate and LT joints. D, Posteroanterior view obtained 8 months after injury. The patient had resumed his racing activities with about a 25% reduction of wrist motion.

Some authors recommend using an external fixator to facilitate exposure while protecting the repair. This is especially indicated in longstanding dislocations with extensive fibrosis. In these cases, several days of progressive joint stretching may facilitate surgery. A description of dual- approach treatment of a perilunate dislocation follows in the Critical Points box.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Perilunate Dislocations: Technique for Open Reduction and Kirschner Wire Fixation (Combined or Dorsal Approach)

Indication
- Perilunate dislocations, unless unstable medical condition contraindicates surgery

Preoperative Evaluation
- Under conscious sedation, the dislocation is reduced in the emergency department (Tavernier’s maneuver) (see Fig. 13.57 ).
- During reduction, traction views are obtained. Following reduction, radio graphs and a CT scan are obtained.
- The forearm is elevated in a well- padded above- elbow splint until deﬁnitive surgery is possible. The patient is counseled on the signs and symptoms of carpal tunnel syndrome, and release of the carpal tunnel is performed as indi cated.

![[Fig.13.57.png]]
> **Fig. 13.57** Schematic representation of Tavernier’s method of reduction of dorsal perilunate dislocations. With the wrist slightly extended, gentle manual traction is applied (1). Without releasing such traction, and while the lunate is stabilized palmarly by the surgeon’s thumb, the wrist is flexed until a reduction clunk occurs (2). This indicates that the proximal pole of the capitate has engaged the dorsal horn of the lunate. At this point, traction is released, and the wrist is brought back to neutral (3).

Surgical Approach
- Perform a dorsal 6- cm straight incision centered on the Lister tubercle.
- Open the extensor retinaculum along the third compartment and expose the second through fourth compartments. Elective neurectomy of the posterior intercarpal nerve may be performed.
- (Optional Palmar approach) Perform an extended carpal tunnel incision, extending proximally across the wrist in a zigzag fashion. Flexor tendons and the median nerve are retracted radially.

Usual Findings
- The dorsal capsule is generally found avulsed off the radius. If the dislocation has not been reduced, an obvious empty space between the capitate and radius appears.
- Exploration of the palmar capsule reveals a consistent arc- like rent, coinciding with the space of Poirier, coursing along the sulcus between the RSC and long RL ligaments, and ulnarly curving across the palmar LT ligament ( Fig. 13.60 ). If the dislocation is unreduced, one can see the distal articular surface of the lunate through this rent.

![[Fig.13.60.png]]
> **Fig. 13.60** A, Schematic representation of the two levels of ligament disruption that are seen with a perilunate dislocation (red arrow) palmarly: the origin of the radioscaphocapitate ligament may have avulsed off the radial styloid (1), and the palmar lunotriquetral ligament often is disrupted through its midsubstance (2). B, Postreduction drawing showing apposition of the torn ligaments.

Reduction and Fixation
- All torn remnants of capsule are removed and the joints are freed from any incarcerated soft tissue.
- The lunate can be easily reduced under direct vision by manually pushing dorsally back in between the capitate and radius, while applying gentle longi tudinal traction on the hand.
- From the dorsum, the carpus is examined for evidence of articular injury.
```
